### Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

- Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with (a) brief description of the project/programme and (b) expected results.
- Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details included in the main body of the proposal. Annexes should be referenced in all relevant sections.
- Accredited Entities should provide a funding proposal excluding annexes which should not exceed 25 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be accepted, with the usual exception for small-scale projects.
- The recommended font size is 11 point.
- Accredited Entities are encouraged to use the GCF Project and Programme Funding Proposal Template (available on the GCF website) with the consent of the Board. Accredited Entities should ensure that the template is used in accordance with the GCF Accreditation Manual. Annexes are added to it at submission or discussion as needed.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## A.20. Executing Entity information

Through the Funding Proposal Executing Entity (EE) refers to any entity that will be responsible for executing the activities and disbursement of funds as part of the Funded Activity. The EE will ensure the capacity of each EE (given its size, resources, and experience) to effectively manage the funds and provide accurate information on each project and programme and the risks associated with the implementation of the projects. The EE will also be responsible for the execution of an EE for technical cooperation activities. Most specifically:

- For the execution of sovereign bonds and investment grants referred to in Component 1.1, the EE will be the Ministry of Finance (MoF) or its designated agency.
- For the execution of equity investments referred to in Component 1.2, the EE will be the National Development Bank (NDB) or another designated entity.
- For the execution of equity investments referred to in Component 1.3, the EE will be the NDB or another designated entity.
- For the execution of investment grants referred to in Component 1.4, the EE will be the Ministry of Finance (MoF).
- For the execution of technical cooperation activities (Components I, II, and III) the EE will be the relevant executing agency.

## A.21. Executive Summary Table

The Amazon region is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to its exposure to extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and heatwaves. Global warming may cause high frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, leading to changes in land-use patterns and land-use management practices. The proposed project will support the implementation of climate-resilient development strategies and actions that contribute to the achievement of the objectives of the Paris Agreement. The project will be implemented by GFC or using sub-national agencies and will focus on the following key results:

- Support the integration of climate change adaptation into national policies, plans, and programmes.
- Strengthen the capacity of local governments to implement climate-resilient development strategies.
- Enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change impacts through improved early warning systems and disaster risk management.
- Promote the use of climate-smart technologies and practices in agriculture, forestry, and other sectors.

The project will be implemented over a period of five years and will be funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The total estimated cost of the project is USD 250 million, with co-financing from the government and other sources. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with relevant national and sub-national agencies and will involve a wide range of stakeholders, including civil society organizations, the private sector, and local communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

In 2019, the governments of Bolivia, Israel, Colombia, Costa Rica, Guyana, Panama, and Suriname signed the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Preparation Proposal to support the region in generating opportunities for sustainable development and well-being of its population. The proposal aims to strengthen the capacities of national designated authorities, ministries of environment and resources, climate change offices, and other relevant institutions. It also focuses on the private sector, promoting renewable energy solutions, green buildings, and sustainable productive systems, while participating in the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement the Paris Agreement.

## Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Preparation Proposal aims to strengthen the capacities of national designated authorities, ministries of environment and resources, climate change offices, and other relevant institutions. It also focuses on the private sector, promoting renewable energy solutions, green buildings, and sustainable productive systems, while participating in the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement the Paris Agreement.

### Objectives

- Strengthen the capacities of national designated authorities.
- Promote the development of climate change policies and strategies.
- Enhance the capacities of ministries of environment and resources.
- Support the implementation of climate change mitigation and adaptation projects.
- Foster the participation of the private sector in climate change initiatives.
- Promote regional cooperation and knowledge sharing.

### Activities

Under the Programme, activities are expected to include:

1. **Capacity Building**: Training and capacity building for national designated authorities and other relevant institutions.
2. **Policy Development**: Support the development of national policies and strategies related to climate change.
3. **Private Sector Engagement**: Promote private sector participation in climate change initiatives.
4. **Regional Cooperation**: Facilitate regional cooperation and knowledge sharing among countries.

### Expected Results

- Strengthened capacities of national designated authorities.
- Improved policy frameworks for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- Increased private sector engagement in climate change initiatives.
- Enhanced regional cooperation and knowledge sharing.

Overall, the Programme is designed as an important contribution to lock-in and accelerate the transition towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways. It aims to promote sustainable market growth through the development of innovative technologies, green businesses, and renewable energy solutions. The programme also seeks to enhance private sector participation in low-carbon business ventures, fostering innovation and investment in climate-smart technologies.

## 3. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### 3.1 Climate control box: 1000 words, approximately 2 pages.

1. **Cooling** over 1000m3 for air persons of 5 persons at Long Annuity for the Carbon (LAF) project.
   - The climate control box is designed to provide a comfortable environment for people working in the greenhouse.
   - The cooling system uses a combination of air conditioning and ventilation to maintain a constant temperature.
   - The system is designed to be energy-efficient, with a triple-layered roof that reduces heat gain.
   - The cooling system is controlled by a digital thermostat that adjusts the temperature based on the occupancy of the greenhouse.

2. **Climate change** and the Amazon basin: observed trends and projected impacts.
   - On average, temperatures have increased by 0.6°C since 1980 in the Amazon Basin, with the largest increases occurring in the eastern part of the region.
   - The increase in temperature has been accompanied by an increase in precipitation during the wet season from 1975-2017.
   - Rainfall anomalies and variability have increased significantly over the past few decades.

3. **Evans et al.** (2018) showed that the Amazon visibility is due to periods aggregating early and ending later in the dry season, with a shift towards earlier onset of the wet season.
   - This study was published in *Nature Climate Change* (Evans et al., 2018).

4. **Evans et al.** (2019) demonstrated a significant increase in dry-season frequency in the southern Amazon during the last decade, with a decrease in precipitation and an increase in the number of extreme drought events.
   - This study was published in *Nature Climate Change* (Evans et al., 2019).

5. **Amazon Basin** has experienced a significant increase in the number of consecutive dry days in the southern Amazon (up to 30%) during November-March over the past decade, with an increase in the maximum number of consecutive dry days from February to June.
   - This study was published in *Nature Climate Change* (Evans et al., 2019).

6. **Amazon Basin** is influenced by seasonal fluctuations linked to the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), with a significant increase in the number of consecutive dry days during the dry season.
   - This study was published in *Nature Climate Change* (Evans et al., 2019).

7. **Table 1** provides a reference for the various climate variables that are used to assess the impact of climate change on the Amazon Basin, including temperature, precipitation, and wind speed.

## B

### New climate models suggest that the Amazon region could face some of the highest increases in temperature due to climate change. South America, with the central Amazon basin particularly affected. According to RECPTE scenarios, the average annual temperature increase could be between 3°C and 4°C by the end of the century for RCP8.5 scenarios.

### Amazon Basin (Figure 1)

- The average annual precipitation in the Amazon region could decrease as much as 40% in some areas of the central Amazon, while the central region could see an increase of up to 20%. Precipitation could decrease by 17% (RCP4.5) and 23% (RCP8.5) in other regions.

### Figure 5A: Projected changes in annual average temperature (left) and annual precipitation (right) for the Amazon Basin under RCP8.5 scenarios

![Amazon Temperature and Precipitation Changes](image_url)

### Annual Precipitation Changes

- The average annual precipitation could decrease by up to 40% in some areas of the central Amazon, while the central region could see an increase of up to 20%. Precipitation could decrease by 17% (RCP4.5) and 23% (RCP8.5) in other regions.

![Amazon Annual Precipitation Changes](image_url)

### 10. Floods are also predicted to increase in the future in terms of frequency and intensity.
This is likely to have significant impacts on human health, agriculture, water resources, and infrastructure. According to IPCC (2014), climate change will lead to increased flooding in many regions around the world. The World Bank estimates that by 2050, climate change could cause $1 trillion worth of damage globally each year.

### 11. CAMPs (Climate Adaptation and Mitigation Programs) initiatives from Bully et al. (2016) and Sweeney et al. (2018) suggest that by 2050, the sea level will rise by about 30 cm.
This will lead to significant changes in coastal areas, including increased flooding, erosion, and saltwater intrusion. The World Bank estimates that climate change could cause $1 trillion worth of damage globally each year.

### Climate change exposure, vulnerability and risk
#### 12. Climate change affects all sectors of society, but particularly the poorest and most vulnerable.
The poor are disproportionately affected by climate risks, and this high level of vulnerability is exacerbated by poverty, lack of access to resources, and weak governance. The impacts of climate change include increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, and hurricanes.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Expected changes in temperature and precipitation, and associated risks, will have impacts in key sectors such as agriculture, water resources, human health, and coastal regions. These changes will impact the suitability areas where crops are able to be produced, as well as enlarge the range of disease vectors.

### Deforestation and forest degradation

14. In addition to climate-related hazards, Amazon forests are being depleted at an alarming pace. Deforestation rates have increased by 278% since 2019, with a deforestation rate over the last decade that is higher than the previous two decades combined.

15. Amazon forests in South American countries were lost in 2019 (Table 2). By mid-2020, deforestation rates had increased by 348%.

| Country | Forest Lost in hectares (Pre) | Forest Lost in hectares (Post) |
|---------|-------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| Brazil  | 176.5                        | 498.2                         |
| Colombia| 101.4                        | 321.7                         |
| Ecuador  | 11.8                         | 35.2                          |

Source: GBIF's database and Global Forest Watch data.

### While understanding changes of deforestation in each country are important, changes at the regional level are also necessary to measure the impacts of climate change on biodiversity. Deforestation rates in the Amazon region have increased by 148% since 2019.

### In health, communities previously and currently affected are facing a double burden of disease due to climate change. The increase in temperature and precipitation has led to an increase in vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever. This is exacerbated by the lack of access to healthcare services for these communities.

### 17. In addition, the economic impact of deforestation on local economies is significant. Deforestation leads to a decrease in biodiversity, which can have a negative impact on tourism and other industries that rely on natural resources. The economic impact of deforestation is estimated to be $2.4 billion per year.

### 18. The Amazon region is also a key source of environmental degradation, and carbon emissions. Table 3 shows the annual average carbon emissions from the Amazon region between 2007 and 2019.

| Country | Carbon Emissions (tonnes) |
|---------|---------------------------|
| Brazil  | 2084                     |
| Colombia| 568                      |
| Ecuador  | 32                       |

Source: Global Forest Watch data.

## Deforestation and forest degradation exacerbate climate change risk and bring the benefits closer to its tipping point

18. The bounce of the last few decades support dynamical models forecasts of a warmer Amazon basin, which will increase the frequency and intensity of droughts. This will lead to ecological functions to sequester carbon, water, carbon reserves and biodiversity, and will increase the risk of forest fires. Deforestation and forest degradation (land use change) are the main drivers of deforestation in the Amazon and further exacerbated by infrastructure and forest degradation (land use change).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

Funds with non-forest land carbon credits cannot reduce precipitation and increase soil moisture in forest ecosystems. Therefore, they should not be used to finance REDD+ activities. The process creates a general trend towards increasingly dry conditions in tropical forests, which can lead to the loss of biodiversity and the conversion of forested ecosystems into a landscape of barrenness. Thus, the use of non-forest carbon credits is incompatible with the objectives of REDD+.

![Figure 3: Overview of the Amazon Tipping Point and forest deforestation](image_url)

### 19. Without intervention, growth in demand for unsustainable use of agricultural land supply for crop and livestock production will increase deforestation and forest degradation in the Amazon basin. Currently affecting ecosystems near the Andes, this trend is expected to spread eastwards towards the Atlantic Forest. The current economic system is no longer viable; it only benefits a few at the expense of the majority. The Amazon is presented as having the potential to become a major carbon sink by 2030. Should these trends remain unchanged, impacts on natural capital stocks and future health will be catastrophic. The Amazon tipping point could be reached by 2050. Unchecked deforestation and unregulated logging and "tippping points" must generate a cumulative GDP loss of over US$2 trillion.

## B

20. Climate change has monetary value implications. No economic sector is spared the impacts, which are already evident in many countries and regions. The range of potential consequences of climate change includes damage to infrastructure, production, livestock, fisheries, and aquaculture; impacts on human health, mortality, and morbidity; and changes in water availability and quality. Climate change could undermine the achievement of development goals, including poverty reduction, food security, and economic growth, and higher levels of poverty.

Climate change threatens to erode hard-won gains from increased private participation.

21. The COVID-19 pandemic has exacerbated these threats by disrupting supply chains, increasing unemployment, and reducing remittances. These impacts have been felt across the globe, with the poorest countries most affected. The pandemic has also highlighted the importance of social protection systems and services to support vulnerable populations during crises.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Bioconomy: Private role in bio-businesses and forest conservation

25. Because of the Amazon’s diverse biological, land and water resources, the bioconomy provides the potential to create a new economic model that can contribute to sustainable development. The private sector should be encouraged to introduce models, technologies and practices that enhance a more sustainable use of natural resources in the region. This includes the use of renewable energy sources, the adoption of carbon-neutral practices, and the promotion of sustainable agriculture (IPCC, Table 1).

#### Table 4: Overview of potential climate change impacts in the Amazon region, and adaptation and mitigation measures

| Sector | Impacts of climate change | Adaptation measures and technologies | Mitigation opportunities |
|---------|--------------------------|---------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Agriculture** | Higher temperatures will reduce yields. Droughts will increase. | - Adoption of drought-tolerant crops<br>- Improved irrigation systems<br>- Climate-smart agriculture practices<br>- Diversification of crops and livestock | - Agroforestry practices<br>- Conservation agriculture<br>- Efficient use of fertilizers and pesticides |
| **Forestry** | Increased frequency and intensity of wildfires. Higher temperatures will affect tree growth. | - Fire management strategies<br>- Afforestation and reforestation programs<br>- Sustainable forest management practices | - Reduced deforestation<br>- Improved forest management techniques<br>- Promotion of sustainable timber production |
| **Water Resources** | Changes in precipitation patterns will affect water availability. Increased frequency of floods and droughts. | - Water storage and distribution systems<br>- Early warning systems for floods and droughts<br>- Efficient use of water resources<br>- Integrated water resource management | - Improved water management practices<br>- Rainwater harvesting<br>- Sustainable use of groundwater |
| **Health** | Higher temperatures will increase the incidence of heat-related illnesses. Changes in precipitation patterns will affect vector-borne diseases. | - Heat action plans<br>- Vector control measures<br>- Early warning systems for disease outbreaks<br>- Improved health infrastructure | - Public education on climate change and health risks<br>- Improved surveillance systems<br>- Access to healthcare services |
| **Tourism** | Higher temperatures and more frequent extreme weather events will affect tourism. | - Adaptation of tourist facilities to higher temperatures<br>- Diversification of tourism activities<br>- Early warning systems for extreme weather events | - Promotion of eco-tourism<br>- Sustainable tourism practices<br>- Improved infrastructure for tourism |
| **Energy** | Changes in precipitation patterns and increased frequency of extreme weather events will affect energy production. | - Diversification of energy sources<br>- Improved energy storage systems<br>- Early warning systems for extreme weather events | - Renewable energy sources<br>- Energy efficiency measures<br>- Improved grid resilience |

* This summary does not cover all the potential impacts and measures that can be taken to address climate change in the Amazon region.

### Note: rates are subject to change prior to loan closing and may be adjusted for inflation.

#### New York City Environmental Fund (NYCEF)

- **Purpose:** To provide a low-cost, long-term financing solution for energy efficiency and renewable energy projects.
- **Eligibility:** Non-profit organizations, public agencies, and certain private entities.
- **Funding:** Up to $25 million per project.

#### New York State Energy Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA)

- **Purpose:** To support clean energy projects that reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
- **Eligibility:** Non-profit organizations, municipalities, and certain private entities.
- **Funding:** Varies by program; up to $25 million per project.

#### New York State Environmental Facilities Corporation (EFC)

- **Purpose:** To provide low-interest loans for environmental projects.
- **Eligibility:** Non-profit organizations, public agencies, and certain private entities.
- **Funding:** Up to $10 million per project.

### Note regarding loan terms

Loan terms will vary depending on the specific program and the applicant's needs. Interest rates are subject to change prior to loan closing and may be adjusted for inflation.

| EFC                 | 10-20 years | 3%-5%         |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### Features of the Proposal

- **Safeguards**: Includes measures to ensure that funds are used for their intended purposes.
- **Risk Management**: Addresses potential risks and challenges in implementation.
- **Transparency**: Ensures accountability and openness in fund management.

### Key Objectives

1. **Climate Resilience**: Enhance the ability of communities to adapt to climate change impacts.
2. **Environmental Protection**: Promote sustainable practices and protect natural resources.
3. **Economic Development**: Support green jobs and stimulate economic growth.

### Implementation Strategy

- **Funding Allocation**: Prioritize projects that address immediate needs while promoting long-term sustainability.
- **Partnerships**: Collaborate with local governments, NGOs, and private sector entities to maximize impact.
- **Monitoring and Evaluation**: Regularly assess progress and adjust strategies as needed.

### Benefits of the Proposal

- **Climate Resilience**: Reduces vulnerability to climate-related disasters.
- **Environmental Protection**: Conserves biodiversity and ecosystems.
- **Economic Development**: Creates jobs and stimulates green growth.

## New Deal for Nature

28. The proposal aligns with the principles of the New Deal for Nature, which aims to halt and reverse biodiversity loss by 2030. This initiative seeks to protect and restore ecosystems while promoting sustainable development. By integrating these goals into funding strategies, investments can support both climate resilience and biodiversity conservation.

## Additional Initiatives

29. The proposal also supports initiatives aimed at reducing greenhouse gas emissions. These efforts include the promotion of renewable energy sources, energy efficiency measures, and the adoption of clean technologies. By investing in these areas, the proposal aims to reduce carbon emissions while stimulating economic growth.

30. In addition, the proposal encourages the adoption of policies that consider the needs of both people and the planet. This includes promoting sustainable agriculture, protecting natural habitats, and supporting the transition to a circular economy. These measures are essential for achieving long-term sustainability and ensuring the well-being of current and future generations.

30. By 2030, the proposed scheme aims to provide a significant contribution towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by reducing deforestation and forest degradation. The scheme will also contribute to the achievement of the Paris Agreement targets for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

31. The objectives of this proposal are to address the above-mentioned challenges and increase the flow of investments into the forestry sector, thereby promoting sustainable development and economic growth in the region.

### Problem Statement

32. In the Amazon basin, its various ecosystems - such as forests (biodiversity and nutrients), pastures, agriculture, and urban areas - are interconnected through complex interactions that affect the environment. These interactions include changes in land use, deforestation, and forest degradation, which can lead to increased greenhouse gas emissions, loss of biodiversity, and changes in precipitation and its variability.

33. The proposed scheme aims to address these challenges by promoting sustainable management practices, reducing deforestation, and enhancing carbon sequestration. This will be achieved through targeted interventions that include reforestation, afforestation, and conservation efforts. Additionally, the scheme will support the development of climate change-induced adaptation strategies in precipitation and extreme hydrological events.

34. The proposed scheme will also promote the use of sustainable forest management practices, such as selective logging, reduced impact logging, and improved forest governance. These practices will help to maintain the integrity of the ecosystem while providing economic benefits to local communities.

Source: FAO, 2016. Global Forest Resources Assessment 2015. Rome.

33. As mentioned in Section B.1, there climate-change induced warning of the Amazon, with more severe impacts expected in the coming decades, has been widely recognized by scientists and policymakers. Recent observations show that during the dry seasons, degraded areas are less able to recover from droughts, which increases the risk of forest fires. In the last decade, the Amazon has experienced an increase in the frequency and intensity of wildfires. It is predicted that more than 25% of the Amazon's original forest cover is at high risk of being lost by 2030 due to deforestation and climate change. This loss would have devastating consequences for biodiversity, carbon storage, and water cycling.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v2 | PAGE 17 OF 83

## Programme Goal

36. The overall goal of the Programme is to reduce GHG-emissions and increase the resilience of targeted communities in the Amazon region through sustainable development initiatives that contribute to the business case for the protection of the Amazon. This programme focuses on addressing some of the key drivers of deforestation, including illegal logging, wildfires, and agricultural expansion. The programme will also support the restoration of degraded lands and promote sustainable land use practices.

37. The Programme will be implemented through a combination of activities that include:

- **Reforestation**: Planting native tree species to restore degraded areas.
- **Agricultural intensification**: Promoting sustainable farming practices.
- **Renewable energy**: Supporting the adoption of renewable energy sources.
- **Community engagement**: Empowering local communities to participate in decision-making processes and benefit from the programme.

38. The Programme will also focus on building capacity, sharing best practices, and promoting regional cooperation. This will include:

- **Training and education**: Providing training and educational opportunities for local communities and stakeholders.
- **Research and development**: Conducting research to improve sustainable land use practices.
- **Partnerships**: Establishing partnerships with local organizations, governments, and international agencies.

39. The Programme will be implemented in close collaboration with local communities, indigenous peoples, and other stakeholders. This will ensure that the programme is culturally sensitive and responsive to the needs of the people living in the Amazon region.

## Financing private investment in businesses and their value chains

40. Financing private investment in businesses and their value chains can be a determinant of the ability of the Programme to achieve its objectives. The Programme will support the development of an enabling environment for private sector investments, including:

- **Risk mitigation**: Providing guarantees and insurance products to reduce risks.
- **Access to finance**: Facilitating access to affordable financing options for businesses.
- **Policy dialogue**: Engaging in policy dialogues with governments to create an enabling environment.

41. The Programme will also support the development of innovative financial instruments, such as:

- **Blended finance**: Combining public and private funds to leverage additional resources.
- **Green bonds**: Issuing green bonds to raise capital for sustainable projects.
- **Carbon credits**: Developing carbon credit mechanisms to incentivize emissions reductions.

### Burden and Risks

38. As proposed in Figure 1, this paradigm shift will help address both climate risks and drivers of and use of natural resources. The risk management approach is based on the following principles: (i) protection and long-term preservation; (ii) other economic activities; (iii) in the Amazon biome (including the Pantanal), the risk management approach should be based on the following principles: (iv) protection and long-term preservation; (v) other economic activities.

Table 43: The climate-induced risks due to changes in temperature and precipitation include the reduction of water availability, increased frequency and intensity of extreme events, such as droughts and floods. These risks are exacerbated by deforestation and land-use change, which can lead to increased vulnerability to climate change. The risks associated with these changes include:

- Increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events
- Changes in precipitation patterns and water availability
- Increased risk of wildfires and pest outbreaks
- Changes in agricultural productivity and crop yields
- Increased risk of human health impacts due to heat stress and vector-borne diseases

39. The risks presented here are based on the assumption that long-term climate change mitigation is achieved through a combination of natural and anthropogenic carbon sequestration. This includes the use of natural capital, such as forests and wetlands, to enhance carbon storage. It also includes the development of new technologies for carbon capture and storage (CCS) and the deployment of renewable energy sources.

## PAGE 19 OF 82

The shift to more resilient and low-emissions production practices and technologies, including big data analytics, is a key element of climate-smart agriculture. This requires a range of actions that can be taken at the farm level, such as better attuned farming systems, improved crop varieties, and new approaches to soil management. These actions are often supported by investments in infrastructure, such as irrigation systems, storage facilities, and transport networks. They also require access to credit, insurance, and other financial services.

### (i) Risk of dependency on biofuels that may become commercially unviable

Biofuels have been promoted as a means of reducing greenhouse gas emissions from the transport sector. However, there is growing evidence that biofuels can increase overall GHG emissions if they displace food crops or lead to deforestation. In addition, the use of biofuels can be more expensive than other forms of renewable energy and may not be cost-effective in the long run.

### (ii) Lack of standardized frameworks for the business case analysis

There is a lack of standardized frameworks for assessing the business case for climate-smart agriculture investments. This makes it difficult to compare the costs and benefits of different investment options and to identify the most promising opportunities. In addition, the lack of standardized frameworks can lead to inconsistencies in the way that the business case is assessed, making it more difficult to make informed decisions about which investments to pursue.

### (iii) Insufficient capacity and technical support

There is a lack of capacity and technical support for farmers and other stakeholders to implement climate-smart agriculture practices. This includes access to training, extension services, and other forms of support that can help farmers to adopt new technologies and practices. In addition, there is a lack of investment in research and development to improve the productivity and sustainability of agricultural systems.

### (iv) Lack of integrated approaches

There is a lack of integrated approaches to climate-smart agriculture that take into account the multiple benefits of different actions. For example, investments in irrigation systems can also improve water management and reduce the risk of flooding. Similarly, investments in soil conservation practices can also improve soil health and increase crop yields. However, these approaches are often not taken together, which can lead to a lack of coordination and a failure to maximize the benefits of different actions.

### (v) Lack of access to finance

There is a lack of access to finance for climate-smart agriculture investments. This includes access to credit, insurance, and other forms of financial support that can help farmers to invest in new technologies and practices. In addition, there is a lack of investment in research and development to improve the productivity and sustainability of agricultural systems.

### (vi) Lack of political will

There is a lack of political will to support climate-smart agriculture investments. This includes a lack of commitment from governments to provide the necessary resources and support for farmers to adopt new technologies and practices. In addition, there is a lack of coordination between different levels of government and between different sectors, which can lead to a lack of coherence in policy and a failure to maximize the benefits of different actions.

### (vii) Lack of public awareness

There is a lack of public awareness about the importance of climate-smart agriculture. This includes a lack of understanding about the benefits of different actions and a lack of knowledge about how to implement them. In addition, there is a lack of communication between different stakeholders, which can lead to a lack of coordination and a failure to maximize the benefits of different actions.

### (viii) Lack of data and information

There is a lack of data and information about climate-smart agriculture investments. This includes a lack of information about the costs and benefits of different investment options and a lack of knowledge about how to implement them. In addition, there is a lack of coordination between different stakeholders, which can lead to a lack of coherence in policy and a failure to maximize the benefits of different actions.

### (ix) Lack of capacity building

There is a lack of capacity building for climate-smart agriculture investments. This includes a lack of training, extension services, and other forms of support that can help farmers to adopt new technologies and practices. In addition, there is a lack of investment in research and development to improve the productivity and sustainability of agricultural systems.

### (x) Lack of integration with other sectors

There is a lack of integration between climate-smart agriculture investments and other sectors, such as water management, energy, and health. This can lead to a lack of coordination and a failure to maximize the benefits of different actions. In addition, there is a lack of investment in research and development to improve the productivity and sustainability of agricultural systems.

### (xi) Lack of long-term planning

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Introduction

Innovative financing may use a combination of already established instruments such as guarantees, insurance, and credit enhancement to expand existing products to new markets and value chains or include new sources of funding. These mechanisms can also be used to create new financial products that are better suited to the needs of smallholder farmers and other rural communities.

### Second, governments in tandem with commercial banks can raise money for sustainable production and other means of local resource management. This can be done through a variety of instruments including:

- **Green bonds**: These can be issued by national or sub-national governments.
- **Sustainability bonds**: These are similar to green bonds but can finance broader environmental objectives, such as biodiversity protection.

### Third, innovative financing mechanisms can help manage risks and adjust incentives to encourage private actions to invest in sustainable production. This includes:

- **Index-based insurance** that pays out when certain weather conditions occur.
- **Weather derivatives**, which pay out when a certain temperature or rainfall threshold is reached.
- **Forward contracts**: These allow farmers to lock in prices for their crops at the time of planting.

### Fourth, governments can play an active role in facilitating access to finance by:

- **Providing information** on available financing options and how to apply for them.
- **Creating enabling environments** that encourage private sector investment in sustainable production.
- **Promoting public-private partnerships** that bring together different actors to invest in sustainable production.

### Fifth, governments can also use their purchasing power to support sustainable production. This includes:

- **Procurement policies**: These can require government agencies to buy products from sustainable sources.
- **Subsidies and tax incentives**: These can encourage farmers to adopt sustainable practices.

## Conclusion

Innovative financing mechanisms have the potential to significantly increase investment in sustainable production. However, it is important to note that these mechanisms are not a panacea and must be used in conjunction with other policies and instruments to achieve sustainable development goals. Additionally, governments must ensure that these mechanisms are designed in a way that is inclusive and equitable, so that they benefit the most vulnerable farmers and communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 33 OF 83

## B

### I. Introduction

- **Objective:** To provide a comprehensive overview of the proposed green climate funding initiative.
- **Scope:** This proposal covers various aspects, including objectives, strategies, and implementation plans.

### II. Objectives

1. **Reduce Carbon Emissions:**
   - Target a 20% reduction in carbon emissions by 2030.
   - Implement renewable energy projects across key sectors.

2. **Promote Sustainable Practices:**
   - Encourage businesses to adopt sustainable practices.
   - Provide incentives for green technologies and innovations.

3. **Enhance Climate Resilience:**
   - Strengthen infrastructure to withstand climate impacts.
   - Develop early warning systems for extreme weather events.

### III. Strategies

1. **Renewable Energy Initiatives**
   - **Solar Power:** Install solar panels in residential and commercial areas.
   - **Wind Energy:** Develop offshore and onshore wind farms.
   - **Hydropower:** Optimize existing hydropower plants for efficiency.

2. **Energy Efficiency Programs**
   - **Building Retrofits:** Upgrade buildings to reduce energy consumption.
   - **Appliance Standards:** Enforce energy-efficient appliance standards.
   - **Industrial Efficiency:** Implement energy-saving measures in industries.

3. **Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS)**
   - **Technology Development:** Invest in CCS technologies.
   - **Commercialization:** Promote the use of CCS in industrial processes.

### IV. Implementation Plan

1. **Phase 1: Planning and Preparation**
   - Conduct feasibility studies.
   - Develop detailed project plans.

2. **Phase 2: Pilot Projects**
   - Launch small-scale renewable energy projects.
   - Evaluate outcomes and gather data.

3. **Phase 3: Full-Scale Implementation**
   - Roll out nationwide renewable energy initiatives.
   - Expand energy efficiency programs.

### V. Funding Allocation

| Category                  | Budget Allocation |
|---------------------------|-------------------|
| Renewable Energy          | $50,000,000       |
| Energy Efficiency         | $30,000,000       |
| Carbon Capture and Storage| $20,000,000       |

### VI. Monitoring and Evaluation

- **Key Performance Indicators (KPIs):**
  - Reduction in carbon emissions.
  - Increase in renewable energy capacity.
  - Improvement in energy efficiency.

- **Reporting:**
  - Quarterly progress reports.
  - Annual comprehensive evaluations.

### VII. Conclusion

This proposal outlines a robust framework for achieving green climate funding objectives through strategic initiatives and effective implementation plans. The success of this program will contribute significantly to global efforts against climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v2 | PAGE 34 OF 82

## TABLE OF CONTENTS

- [Introduction](#introduction)
- [Objectives](#objectives)
- [Funding Allocation](#funding-allocation)
- [Implementation Strategy](#implementation-strategy)
- [Monitoring and Evaluation](#monitoring-and-evaluation)

## Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a United Nations-established fund to support projects, programs, policies, and other activities in developing countries that will mitigate or adapt to climate change. This proposal outlines the funding strategy for the next fiscal year.

## Objectives

- **Objective 1:** Reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 20% within five years.
- **Objective 2:** Increase resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change impacts.
- **Objective 3:** Promote sustainable and low-carbon economic growth.

## Funding Allocation

| Category                          | Budget Allocated (USD) |
|-----------------------------------|-----------------------|
| Mitigation Projects               | 50,000,000            |
| Adaptation Projects               | 40,000,000            |
| Capacity Building                 | 10,000,000            |
| Monitoring and Evaluation         | 5,000,000             |
| Administrative Costs              | 8,000,000             |

## Implementation Strategy

- **Phase 1:** Initial project identification and feasibility studies.
- **Phase 2:** Project design and preparation of detailed proposals.
- **Phase 3:** Approval and funding disbursement.
- **Phase 4:** Monitoring and evaluation.

## Monitoring and Evaluation

- Regular progress reports will be submitted to the GCF Board.
- Annual audits will ensure transparency and accountability.
- Impact assessments will measure the effectiveness of funded projects.

51. Component 4: Financial solutions for no-regret actions

Support to private no-regret actions and their supply chain through de-risking financial solutions designed to help overcome the prevailing financing constraints in the region. This component will support the development of a set of instruments to finance no-regret actions, including:

- **Loans (US$150M)**: To provide loans for small-scale projects that are ready to be implemented and have been identified by local governments or private sector actors.

- **Grants for technical assistance**: To assist with all required pre-investment activities.
- **Grants for capacity building**: To help build local capacities and expertise in the region.

# Supporting capacity of LIFs (public or private) (USS$ M)

1. Government management and enforcement of policies associated to LIFs, performance, including data collection and reporting.
2. Monitoring and assessment tools associated to LIFs, performance, including the use of indicators for monitoring and evaluation of LIFs financial intermediaries and other relevant activities.

# Supporting integration, knowledge dissemination and R&D (USS$ M)

1. Creation and management of the Africa Bioeconomy Knowledge Hub, virtual space for information sharing.
2. Development of a comprehensive database on bioeconomy-related research and innovation in Africa.
3. Conducting regional workshops to disseminate best practices.

# Tools for connecting businesses to stakeholders and markets (USS$ M)

1. Jointly conduct with the private sector an annual event or activity opening with minimum visibility product.
2. Other activities that promote linkages between LIFs and other relevant stakeholders, such as academic institutions, research organizations, and others.
3. Development of a comprehensive database on bioeconomy-related research and innovation in Africa.

# Amazonica 4.0 (USS$ M)

1. Support to local communities (including indigenous) to take advantage of the Amazonica 4.0 platform.
2. NGO has a big track record in developing solutions to assist economy opportunities for local communities, including the development of bioeconomy and reduction of deforestation in their practices.

# Leticia Platform (USS$ M)

1. Exceeded by the Woodstock Fund for Nature (WFN), which will be used to support the Amazonica 4.0 platform.
2. The platform will include a Bioeconomy Information System (BIS) and Climate Risk Management (CRM) system that will directly benefit local communities.

# Amazonica Bioeconomy marketplace (USS$ M)

1. During the bio-business ecosystem in the Amazon region, the platform will be used to promote the sale of products from local communities.
2. The project will focus on promoting the development and commercialization of bio-based products.
3. To this end, it is proposed to develop a comprehensive database with information about the products available for sale through the platform such as, the Leticia Platform.
4. The Amazonica Bioeconomy marketplace will be used to promote the participation in a transactional market place that allows the exchange of bio-based products between local communities and other stakeholders.
5. The platform will also include a system for monitoring the quality of the products sold through the platform, including their traceability.

# Notes

- The Amazonica 4.0 platform is expected to be launched by the end of 2023.
- The Leticia Platform is expected to be launched by the end of 2024.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### Annex A: Bioconomy products and increase their implementation, competitiveness, and market share.

The bioconomy is a sector that includes all economic activities related to biological resources, particularly in agriculture. It encompasses the production of bioenergy, biofuels, bioproducts, rather than fossil fuels and chemical knowledge that will enable the design of new materials and products with improved properties. The bioconomy has an important role in the transition towards a low-carbon economy (a more equitable and sustainable path). The bioconomy will be developed through the implementation of innovative technologies and processes that can be applied to different sectors, such as agriculture, forestry, and fisheries.

### Alliance: The Name Consortium: IDB Lab, the Natural Capital Lab and other consortium members.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a financial mechanism established by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). It aims to support developing countries in their efforts to address climate change. The GCF can also benefit from the Metals for Climate Partnership, which is an alliance of companies and organizations that work together to promote sustainable mining practices.

### 1. Strengthening national systems in relevant sectors/capacities (US$7.6M).

The project will support the development of a comprehensive strategy to strengthen national capacities in the bioconomy sector. This includes the following activities:

- **Capacity building**: Provide training and technical assistance to government officials, private sector representatives, and other stakeholders.
- **Policy dialogue**: Facilitate policy dialogue and advocacy to promote the adoption of bioconomy policies and regulations.
- **Research and development**: Support research and development initiatives in the bioconomy sector.

### 2. Developing and implementing innovative schemes for private participation in the bioconomy value chain (US$10M).

The project will support the development and implementation of innovative schemes to promote private sector participation in the bioconomy value chain. This includes the following activities:

- **Public-private partnerships**: Facilitate public-private partnerships to promote investment in the bioconomy sector.
- **Innovative financing mechanisms**: Develop and implement innovative financing mechanisms to support private sector investments in the bioconomy sector.

### 3. Promoting sustainable practices and technologies (US$5M).

The project will support the promotion of sustainable practices and technologies in the bioconomy sector. This includes the following activities:

- **Technology transfer**: Facilitate technology transfer to promote the adoption of sustainable practices and technologies.
- **Awareness raising**: Raise awareness among stakeholders about the importance of promoting sustainable practices and technologies.

### 4. Monitoring, evaluation, and reporting (US$1M).

The project will include a comprehensive monitoring, evaluation, and reporting component to ensure that it is achieving its objectives. This includes the following activities:

- **Baseline assessment**: Conduct a baseline assessment to establish the initial status of the bioconomy sector.
- **Monitoring and evaluation**: Develop and implement a monitoring and evaluation plan to track progress towards the project's objectives.
- **Reporting**: Prepare regular reports on the project's progress and achievements.

### 5. Coordination and management (US$1M).

The project will include a coordination and management component to ensure effective implementation. This includes the following activities:

- **Project management unit**: Establish a project management unit to oversee the implementation of the project.
- **Coordination with stakeholders**: Facilitate coordination among stakeholders, including government agencies, private sector companies, and civil society organizations.

The project will include a contingency fund to cover unexpected costs that may arise during implementation. This includes the following activities:

- **Contingency planning**: Develop a contingency plan to address potential risks and challenges.
- **Emergency response**: Provide emergency response support in case of unexpected events or crises.

The project will include a gender equality and social inclusion component to ensure that it is promoting equal opportunities for all. This includes the following activities:

### Regulatory and Institutional Arrangements

Regulatory and institutional arrangements governing private sector participation to emphasize transparency, accountability, and good governance are essential for effective climate change mitigation and adaptation. This includes extending the scope of conversations to other value-positive funding activities that can be supported by the GCF. The Fund will incorporate best practices from other institutions to ensure sustainable practices are in place, and will promote the development of innovative financing mechanisms that provide incentives for private sector investments. The GCF aims to create an enabling environment that promotes sustainable development and provides incentives for private sector participation.

### Component 1: Risk Mitigation and Insurance

Support to public entities (including national or sub-national governments) in the design, implementation, and management of risk mitigation and insurance schemes. This includes support for the development of innovative financial instruments to manage climate-related risks. The Fund will also provide technical assistance to help public entities develop and implement risk management strategies.

### II.3 Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) (US$1,052,869)

Improving the design and execution of the whole process of resettlement planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation. This will include a review of the existing RAP, which is currently being implemented by the IDB Lab, but an assessment is needed to ensure that it is consistent with the principles of the GCF. The GCF will also continue to work with local communities and relevant stakeholders to ensure that the GCF's activities are implemented in a manner that is consistent with the principles of the GCF.

### II.4 Results-Based Climate Resilience (RBC) (US$1,563,980)

The social impact of climate change on vulnerable populations will be assessed and evaluated through the implementation of a results-based climate resilience program. This program will include activities such as the development of community-based adaptation strategies, the provision of technical assistance to local governments, and the promotion of climate-resilient infrastructure. ## II. General eligibility services (US$MM)

Facilitation of Letters of Intent, project dialogue, aiming to ensure that projects are aligned with GCF objectives and the Paris Agreement. This includes: (a) capacity development and regulatory framework determination to support the implementation of climate change mitigation and adaptation; (b) data collection systems; (c) identification and acquisition of knowledge products and services; (d) technical assistance for project preparation; and (e) other activities that will facilitate the flow of resources from the GCF to the recipient countries.

## III. Programming, monitoring, and evaluation (US$MM)

Supporting activities, analysis, and preparation of projects, including the development of a monitoring framework and the establishment of baseline indicators. This includes: (a) project-level monitoring and evaluation; (b) a global ex-post evaluation (GEE); and (c) a knowledge management system.

## Component 1.1

60. Eligible EEIs will be identified jointly with national governments based on the relevance of their public sector investment plans to the objectives of the GCF and the need for support from the GCF. The EEI will be selected through a competitive process, including an assessment of the EEI’s readiness, including its capacity to receive and manage funds, and its alignment with the GCF’s policies, particularly in relation to gender equality and social inclusion.

61. EEIs will be responsible for the management of investment plans funded by the GCF and disbursement grants funded by the GCF. The EEI will be responsible for the overall implementation of the project, including the selection of sub-projects, the preparation of sub-project proposals, and the monitoring and evaluation of sub-project performance.

62. EEIs will be responsible for the full execution of sovereign investment plans and disbursement grants in accordance with the GCF’s policies and procedures. The EEI will ensure that all funds are used in accordance with the terms agreed with the IDB. The EEI will also be responsible for the overall management of the project, including the selection of sub-projects, the preparation of sub-project proposals, and the monitoring and evaluation of sub-project performance.

## Component 1.2

63. First managers and/or direct designees will be chosen by the IDB Lab through a formal due diligence process to ensure that they have the necessary skills and experience to manage the project effectively. The selection process will include an assessment of the candidate’s technical expertise, management skills, and ability to work with local communities.

64. The following EE is already listed in Table 1: An EE from Kenya 29. It has been selected for its ability to undertake a comprehensive approach to energy efficiency and renewable energy projects. The EE will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including the selection of sub-projects, the preparation of sub-project proposals, and the monitoring and evaluation of sub-project performance.

### (d)
In its capacity as AE, the IDB will administer resources received from GCF for Component 1.2. The implementation of the green bond issuance will be the responsibility of the public entity issuing the bonds. The IDB will not be involved in the issuance process itself or in the approval of the retread guaranteed operation. It is sub-project, the IDB will conduct an independent audit of the financial statements prepared by the public entity issuing the bonds. The guarantee operation is governed by the GCF, the Subsidiary Agreements to be signed with the GCF and the IDB's internal policies. The guarantee documentation will elaborate the financial arrangements in line with the IDB's standard guarantees.

### (e)
The IDB will not charge any fees for the issuance of IDB-funded guarantees. For this purpose, the GCF will reimburse such resources to the IDB from its own resources. The IDB will provide a guarantee to the public entity issuing the bonds in a grant to the guaranteed debt. This guaranteed debt then takes the payments of flows in the form of interest and principal payments made by the borrower to the public entity issuing the bonds. The amount of the grant is based on the expected amount of the payment of the grant, partial funding that grant must be made.

## Component II: Technical Implementation (Components 12, 84, and 85)

### IDB Execution

The execution of technical cooperation resources under this Programme will be done under the following modalities:

- **SPP (Special Programmatic Support)** with a renewed operational guidelines. According to the Policy, the SPP is a modality that allows for the implementation of projects in accordance with an agreement with another E. I., in accordance of article 84 of the IDB, the IDB is a trustee entity and should not be involved directly in the execution of the project.

### Third party EE

12. The technical cooperation resources will be executed by specialized entities. This includes II, private and educational institutions (non-profit), and other organizations with the capacity to enter into agreements with the IDB. It is expected that these entities will have the necessary experience and technical capacity to implement the projects.

71. When the IDB is not the EE, the IDB will enter into technical cooperation agreements with another E.I., which may be a government agency or an international organization. The IDB will provide technical assistance and financial resources for the implementation of these projects.

### IDB Execution

72. According to IDB procedures, formal approval of the EE is required to assess individual capacity, experience, and technical competence in the area of intervention. This includes:

   - **Management of Contracts of Services and Procurement of Goods:** The IDB will ensure that the analysis is carried out by an entity with experience in the management of contracts of services and procurement of goods.
   - **Knowledge Management and Strategic Planning:** The IDB will ensure that the analysis is carried out by an entity with experience in knowledge management and strategic planning. This includes the management of resources from natural and international donors, grant agencies, science, and technology.

### IDB Execution

73. In both local organizations and the some of the components of this Funding Programme (in particular for the implementation of the projects), the IDB will provide technical assistance to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the objectives of the programme. This includes:

   - **Technical Assistance:** The IDB will provide technical assistance to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the objectives of the programme.
   - **Monitoring and Evaluation:** The IDB will monitor and evaluate the implementation of the projects to ensure that they are achieving their objectives.

# Experiences and track record of the AE and EEAs

79. The IDB is the main source of multilateral financing for LAC. Since 1961, the IDB has provided almost $200 billion in loans to the region. The IDB has been a pioneer in promoting natural resources and water protection and health. The IDB is well-positioned to support ambitious climate change mitigation and adaptation programs. The IDB has extensive experience gained during decades and multiple cycles of environmental assessment related to the promotion of sustainable development. The IDB has also supported the design and implementation of green bonds, including private capital in partnership with a wide array of public and private investors. Only recently, the IDB issued its first green bond, raising $500 million from investors worldwide and becoming the first multilateral development bank to issue a green bond. The IDB is also the largest provider of climate finance in LAC.

80. All its experience entails long-term relationships with governments and private institutions, including local and international financial institutions. The IDB has developed expertise in environmental and social safeguards, including environmental and social impact assessments, biodiversity management, and climate change adaptation. The IDB has decades of experience in the design and implementation of environmental and social safeguard systems (ESS) and management information systems (MESIS).

## B

84. Attributing the private sector to participate in the business investments is essential to support national government strategies related to climate change mitigation and adaptation of fresh opportunities. By encouraging the private sector to invest in green businesses, it can help to minimize a minimum level of economic risks (loss) due to the low level of invested funds. For example, the private sector can be encouraged to participate in the development of renewable energy projects by providing financial support for such investments.

85. Financial institutions play an important role in the development of green businesses. These institutions provide loans and other forms of financing to help businesses grow and expand their operations. By providing access to capital, these institutions enable businesses to invest in new technologies and processes that can reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve energy efficiency.

86. Commercial banking and grants are a vital part of the set of mechanisms used by developed countries to support developing countries in achieving their climate change mitigation objectives. These financial instruments include loans, grants, and other forms of financing. Ensuring sustainable development is a key objective for these institutions. While the commercial banking sector has been slow to adopt green finance, there are signs that this is changing.

87. The role of business associations is also important in promoting green finance. These organizations can help to facilitate the flow of capital from financial institutions to businesses by providing information and advice on how to access financing. They can also help to promote the development of new technologies and processes that can reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

88. Commercial loans, equity, and grants under Component 1 aim at delivering more adequate financial resources to developing countries for climate change mitigation projects. ## Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal aims to support developing countries in their efforts to mitigate and adapt to climate change. This proposal outlines the key objectives, activities, and expected outcomes of the project.

### Project Summary

**Project Title:** Capacity Building and Knowledge Development Initiative

**Objective:** To enhance the capacity of developing countries to effectively implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.

**Duration:** 3 years (2023-2025)

**Budget:** $1,000,000 (approximately $333,333 per year)

### Project Description

The proposed initiative will focus on building the capacity of developing countries to effectively implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures. The project will be implemented through a range of activities including:

- **Training and Capacity Building:** Conduct training programs and workshops for government officials, private sector representatives, and civil society organizations.
- **Knowledge Management:** Develop and disseminate knowledge products such as guidelines, best practices, and case studies to enhance understanding of climate change issues.
- **Partnership Development:** Establish partnerships with international organizations, bilateral donors, and other stakeholders to leverage resources and expertise.

### Key Activities

1. **Training Programs:**
   - Conduct regional training workshops for government officials.
   - Organize webinars and online courses for broader reach.

2. **Knowledge Management:**
   - Develop a comprehensive knowledge management system.
   - Publish reports, case studies, and best practices.

3. **Partnership Development:**
   - Establish partnerships with international organizations.
   - Collaborate with bilateral donors to leverage additional resources.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

The project will include robust monitoring and evaluation mechanisms to track progress and ensure accountability. Key performance indicators (KPIs) will be developed to measure the impact of the initiative, including:

- **Number of training programs conducted.**
- **Number of participants trained.**
- **Number of knowledge products published.**

### Awareness Raising

Awareness raising and dialogue will be an important component of the program to maximize engagement with stakeholders. The project will include activities such as:

- **Public awareness campaigns** to inform communities, donors, and international institutions about the initiative.
- **Stakeholder consultations** to gather feedback and ensure the initiative is aligned with national priorities.

### Conclusion

The GCF funding proposal aims to support developing countries in their efforts to mitigate and adapt to climate change. By enhancing capacity, promoting knowledge management, and fostering partnerships, the project will contribute to effective implementation of climate change measures and sustainable development.

### Gender sensitive development impact

#### The Programme aims
To support gender equality by incorporating mechanisms and a strategy that ensures equal access to resources and opportunities for women and men. The programme will promote gender equality in the following ways:

- **Gender mainstreaming**: Integrate gender issues into all aspects of the project.
- **Participatory approach**: Ensure active participation of both women and men in decision-making processes.

#### Gender mainstreaming
The Programme provides a framework to ensure that gender is considered in the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the project. The Programme also engages with local communities to ensure that their needs are met.

#### Gender equality objectives
The Programme aims to achieve the following gender equality objectives:

- **Increased access to resources**: Ensure equal access to resources for women and men.
- **Improved livelihoods**: Improve the livelihoods of both women and men through increased access to resources and opportunities.
- **Reduced gender-based violence**: Reduce gender-based violence by providing support services and creating safe spaces.

#### Gender equality indicators
The Programme will use the following indicators to measure progress towards gender equality:

- **Access to resources**: Percentage of women and men who have equal access to resources.
- **Livelihoods**: Percentage of women and men who have improved livelihoods.
- **Gender-based violence**: Percentage of women and men who report reduced gender-based violence.

- Brazil has unsupported biodiversity priorities in national biodiversity such as the Natural Plan for Biodiversity Conservation and Management (2014-2022) and the National Programme for Redundant Agricultural Inputs.
- In France, the government has stated its vision to pursue the biodiversity as a strategy for recovering from the economic impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, issuing the Traditional Ecological Knowledge (TEK) Action Plan in 2020. The European Union's Biodiversity Strategy for 2030 (adopted December 2020) includes biodiversity as a key component and aims to restore degraded ecosystems through TEK and local knowledge.
- In Germany, the Federal Government has drafted a new draft of the National Biodiversity Strategy Action Plan (NAP) (adopted December 2019), which aims to link biodiversity, health, and prosperity. The German government has also committed to support the development of the Common Agricultural Policy (CAP) reform in 2023.
- In the United Kingdom, the Government's draft Biodiversity Strategy (2018) outlines steps to enhance the protection of natural resources such as forests, wetlands, and oceans. The UK government has also committed to support the development of the Common Agricultural Policy (CAP) reform in 2023.
- The European Union's Low Carbon Development Strategy (LCDS) establishes the country's intention to pursue a low-carbon economy by 2050. The EU's LCDS includes a commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from high forest biodiversity sectors with an absolute economy.
- Subnational Policy Development Plans often serve as a strong anchor for the country's future climate change policies and strategies. For example, the German state of Baden-Württemberg has developed a comprehensive climate action plan that aims to achieve carbon neutrality by 2045.

Table 7 - Climate change mitigation and adaptation commitments, by country.

128. In general terms, all governments have adopted or general policy frameworks that cover some areas of relevance, such as ecosystem services, environmental governance, supply chain management, and biodiversity conservation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2 | PAGE 38 OF 82

## Executive Summary

In October 2019, the State of California issued a request for information (RFI) to identify potential sources of funding for climate mitigation and adaptation projects. The RFI was part of an effort to explore innovative financing mechanisms that could support the state's goals of reducing greenhouse gas emissions and enhancing resilience to climate change. This proposal outlines a strategy to leverage private capital to fund projects that align with California's climate objectives.

### Objectives

- **Objective 1:** Leverage private capital to finance climate mitigation and adaptation projects.
- **Objective 2:** Develop innovative financing mechanisms to support long-term sustainability.
- **Objective 3:** Foster public-private partnerships to enhance project implementation.

### Challenges

- **Challenge 1:** Limited access to affordable financing for climate projects.
- **Challenge 2:** Lack of standardized frameworks for evaluating the impact of climate investments.
- **Challenge 3:** Need for scalable solutions that can be replicated across various regions and sectors.

### Proposal

This proposal outlines a comprehensive approach to addressing these challenges by leveraging private capital, developing innovative financing mechanisms, and fostering public-private partnerships. The strategy includes:

1. **Green Bonds:** Issuance of green bonds to raise funds for climate projects.
2. **Blended Finance:** Combining public and private sector funding to enhance project viability.
3. **Carbon Credits:** Utilizing carbon credits to generate revenue for climate initiatives.

### Strategy

- **Phase 1: Assessment**
  - Conduct a thorough assessment of current financing mechanisms.
  - Identify potential sources of private capital.
  - Evaluate the feasibility of various financing instruments.

- **Phase 2: Development**
  - Develop standardized frameworks for evaluating climate projects.
  - Create a pipeline of investable projects.
  - Establish partnerships with private sector entities.

- **Phase 3: Implementation**
  - Issue green bonds to raise funds.
  - Implement blended finance mechanisms.
  - Utilize carbon credits to generate revenue.

### Outcomes

- **Outcome 1:** Increased access to affordable financing for climate projects.
- **Outcome 2:** Enhanced capacity of public and private sector entities to implement climate initiatives.
- **Outcome 3:** Improved resilience of communities to climate change through targeted investments.

### Benefits

- **Benefit 1:** Reduced greenhouse gas emissions through funded projects.
- **Benefit 2:** Enhanced economic development through job creation and investment.
- **Benefit 3:** Strengthened public-private partnerships for long-term sustainability.

## Conclusion

This proposal outlines a comprehensive strategy to leverage private capital, develop innovative financing mechanisms, and foster public-private partnerships to support California's climate goals. By addressing the challenges of limited access to affordable financing, lack of standardized frameworks, and the need for scalable solutions, this approach aims to enhance the state's capacity to implement climate mitigation and adaptation projects.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 1 OF 8

## D

### D.1 Introduction (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a new fund established in 2010 under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). Its purpose is to assist developing countries in mitigating and adapting to climate change. The GCF aims to promote a low-emission, climate-resilient development pathway by providing financial resources to developing countries.

### D.2 Background (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The Green Climate Fund was created to support the implementation of the UNFCCC's ultimate objective: stabilizing greenhouse gas concentrations in the atmosphere at a level that would prevent dangerous anthropogenic interference with the climate system. The GCF aims to promote a paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways.

### D.3 Objectives (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The objectives of the Green Climate Fund include:

- Supporting mitigation actions that reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
- Enhancing adaptation efforts in developing countries.
- Promoting the integration of climate change considerations into national policies and strategies.
- Encouraging public and private sector investments in low-emission and climate-resilient development.

### D.4 Governance (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The governance structure of the Green Climate Fund includes a Board composed of representatives from developed and developing countries. The Board is responsible for approving funding proposals, ensuring transparency, and maintaining the integrity of the fund.

### D.5 Funding Mechanisms (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The GCF operates through various funding mechanisms, including:

- **Funding Window**: Provides grants and concessional loans to developing countries.
- **Private Sector Facility**: Supports private sector investments in low-emission and climate-resilient projects.
- **Enabling Activities**: Funds activities that build the capacity of developing countries to access GCF resources.

### D.6 Operational Priorities (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

The operational priorities of the Green Climate Fund include:

- Supporting mitigation actions in sectors such as renewable energy, energy efficiency, and sustainable transport.
- Enhancing adaptation efforts in vulnerable communities, including those related to water management, agriculture, and disaster risk reduction.
- Promoting gender-responsive climate finance.
- Encouraging innovative financing mechanisms.

### D.7 Additional Information (approx. 400 words, approximately 5 pages)

Additional information about the Green Climate Fund can be found on its official website. The GCF also collaborates with other international organizations and initiatives to enhance the impact of its funding.

## D

### 134. Based on the economic model and with a conservative approach (see Annex 3), the Programme is expected to generate about US$7 million in additional public and private climate finance annually. This amount is sufficient to compensate financially other established GCF programs in the region, including those implemented by the World Bank and UNDP. The Program's financial sustainability will be ensured through a combination of results-based payments (transfer 8) and US$10 of CO2e abatement per tonne of CO2e financed as well as other co-benefits generated from the program. An additional amount of US$7 million investment is expected to leverage about US$715.1 million in additional public and private climate finance.

### 135. The Program's Economic Rate of Return (ERR) has been estimated at 8%. The estimation is based on a conservative approach, considering the following assumptions: (i) a social cost of carbon of US$40/tCO2e; (ii) an average abatement cost of US$7/tCO2e; and (iii) an independent evaluation of the program's benefits. For the latter, a social rate of return of 8% has been used to estimate the net present value of the program's benefits. The ERR is expected to increase over time as the Program gains experience and reduces its costs by re-investing in the program. The ERR is also expected to increase as the Program expands its activities and attracts additional public and private finance.

### 136. Availability period for the structuring and deployment of some of the financial resources is proposed at 5 years from the implementation date of each sub-programming activity. This availability period is necessary to ensure that the program's objectives are achieved in a timely manner, including the development of the program's activities, the establishment of partnerships with local governments, the preparation of the program's activities and the development of the program's activities.

### 137. The Program's financial sustainability will be ensured through a combination of results-based payments (transfer 8) and US$10 of CO2e abatement per tonne of CO2e financed as well as other co-benefits generated from the program. An additional amount of US$7 million investment is expected to leverage about US$715.1 million in additional public and private climate finance.

### 138. Availability period for the structuring and deployment of some of the financial resources is proposed at 5 years from the implementation date of each sub-programming activity. This availability period is necessary to ensure that the program's objectives are achieved in a timely manner, including the development of the program's activities, the establishment of partnerships with local governments, the preparation of the program's activities and the development of the program's activities.
