## E

### Funded by

**U.N. Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO)**

- **Project Title:** Enhancing Market Access for Sustainable and Inclusive Trade in Green Climate Fund (GCF) Accredited National Designated Authorities (NDAs)
- **Location:** Global
- **Amount Requested:** USD 14,025,000

### Project Description

**Objective:**
To enhance market access for sustainable and inclusive trade in green climate fund accredited national designated authorities.

**Activities:**
- Strengthen the capacity of NDAs to effectively engage with private sector actors.
- Develop and implement strategies to promote sustainable trade practices.
- Facilitate partnerships between NDAs and private sector entities.
- Provide technical assistance and training to enhance NDA capabilities.
- Promote policy dialogue and advocacy for sustainable trade.

**U.N. Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO)**

- **Project Title:** Enhancing Market Access for Sustainable and Inclusive Trade in Green Climate Fund (GCF) Accredited National Designated Authorities (NDAs)

### Project Description

**Objective:**
To enhance market access for sustainable and inclusive trade in green climate fund accredited national designated authorities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## 1. Introduction and regulatory framework

Historical context and existing legal/regulatory frameworks for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### 1.1 Objectives

- Strengthening national regulatory frameworks.
- Enhancing institutional capacities to implement climate change policies.
- Promoting regional cooperation in climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- Encouraging private sector participation in climate change initiatives.
- Ensuring transparency, accountability, and effectiveness of climate change actions.

## 2. Organizational Structure

### 2.1 Governance

Organizational structure to ensure effective implementation and oversight.

### 2.2 Management

Management strategies for efficient operation and decision-making processes.

## 3. Funding Mechanisms

### 3.1 Allocation of Funds

- Direct allocation from government budgets.
- International aid and grants.
- Private sector investments.
- Innovative financing mechanisms (e.g., carbon trading, green bonds).

### 3.2 Implementation

- Streamlined administrative processes to expedite fund disbursement.
- Clear guidelines for project selection and approval.
- Regular monitoring and evaluation of funded projects.

## 4. Project Prioritization

### 4.1 Selection Criteria

Criteria used to select projects for funding, including:

- Environmental impact.
- Economic viability.
- Social benefits.
- Feasibility and scalability.

### 4.2 Implementation Plan

Detailed plan outlining steps for project implementation, including:

- Preparation of feasibility studies.
- Development of detailed project plans.
- Allocation of resources (human, financial).
- Regular updates on project progress.

## 5. Monitoring and Evaluation

### 5.1 Indicators

Key performance indicators to measure the success of funded projects, such as:

- Reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.
- Increase in renewable energy usage.
- Improvement in climate resilience.
- Enhanced public awareness and engagement.

### 5.2 Reporting

Regular reporting on project progress and outcomes, including:

- Annual reports.
- Progress updates.
- Impact assessments.
- Lessons learned and best practices.

## 6. Capacity Building

### 6.1 Training Programs

Training programs to enhance the skills of stakeholders involved in climate change initiatives, such as:

- Technical training.
- Policy development workshops.
- Best practice exchanges.

### 6.2 Knowledge Sharing

Platforms for knowledge sharing among stakeholders, including:

- Conferences and seminars.
- Online forums and webinars.
- Peer-to-peer learning networks.

## 7. Stakeholder Engagement

### 7.1 Public Participation

Mechanisms to engage the public in climate change initiatives, such as:

- Public consultations.
- Community meetings.
- Feedback loops for continuous improvement.

### 7.2 Private Sector Involvement

Strategies to involve the private sector in climate change efforts, including:

- Partnerships with businesses.
- Green investment opportunities.
- Incentives for sustainable practices.

## 8. Risk Management

### 8.1 Identification

Identification of potential risks associated with climate change initiatives, such as:

- Financial risks.
- Operational risks.
- Environmental risks.

### 8.2 Mitigation Strategies

Strategies to mitigate identified risks, including:

- Insurance mechanisms.
- Contingency plans.
- Diversified funding sources.

## 9. Conclusion

Summary of the key points discussed in the proposal and the importance of effective climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies.

- Identify risks
- Assess risks
- Develop risk management strategies
- Implement risk management strategies

#### EE organizational structure, internal processes, human resources, systems, goods and services
- Project, technical design, planning, sustainability, governance system, environment and social safeguards
- Procurement, finance, IT, HR, legal, compliance, audit, natural, social

### F.3 Some of the risks and impact of each risk are defined as:
- For probability: high, medium-high, medium-low, low, very low
  - For impact: high, medium-high, medium-low, low, very low

#### F.4 The GBS Risk Management Framework is used to undertake a thorough environmental and social safeguards (ESG) risk assessment for all sub-programs. ESG risks are identified in compliance with the GCF Social and Environmental Safeguards Policy. Each opportunity is assessed against the following criteria:
- Avoidance
- Mitigation
- Acceptance

  - **Technical and operational**: Medium

  - **Technical and operational**: Medium
  - **Disruption**: Medium

### Aim of supporting firms to develop high quality proposals that are bankable. The individual capacity building component will be delivered through a series of workshops and training sessions. | Technical and operational | High |

### 150. Because the technology is a relatively new concept that involves various specific technical skills, it may not be fully deployed in all target countries. The capacity of local institutions to deliver the required services will vary from country to country but are not usually skilled to deliver geophysical data or technical aspects of specific technologies. This may lead to delays and inefficiencies in the implementation process. It is also likely that existing risk management systems and procedures will need to be adapted to accommodate new risks. The capacity of local institutions to deliver the required services will vary from country to country but are not usually skilled to deliver geophysical data or technical aspects of specific technologies. This may lead to delays and inefficiencies in the implementation process. It is also likely that existing risk management systems and procedures will need to be adapted to accommodate new risks.

### 151. As part of the implementation activities, local GCF experts from the Programme specific monitoring and reporting arrangements will be established, including templates and indicators, made available to all countries. This will ensure that the capacity building component is fully integrated into the overall programme design. The capacity building component will also include a review of existing risk management systems and procedures in place within each country that includes assessment of current risk management activities and the implementation of new risk management arrangements.

#### Selective Risk Factor 7: Unforeseen changes in political will

| **Probability** | **Impact** |
| --- | --- |
| Low | Moderate |

### 152. Given the large developments around the COVID-19 pandemic, a new risk factor has been identified as a result of this. This is a risk factor that is not usually considered in the context of the programme design. The region has developed a strong capacity to deliver on the objectives of the programme and the capacity building component will be fully integrated into the overall programme design. The capacity building component will also include a review of existing risk management systems and procedures in place within each country that includes assessment of current risk management activities and the implementation of new risk management arrangements.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 1 OF 8

## Selected Risk Factor E: Minimal of materials or technology procured for unachieved, higher or misdirected use

### Risk Category: Procurement

| Probability | Impact |
|-------------|--------|
| Low         | Medium |

#### Description
Due to the diverse nature of the livelihoods systems, materials, practices and technologies used, funds may be spent on items that are not needed. This can occur when procurement is not properly planned or when there is a lack of coordination between different components of the project. This can also happen if the procurement process is not transparent.

### Mitigation Measures

1. **EE: Eligibility and Subsidy**
   - Ensure that all funds are spent in accordance with individual capability assessments and formal agreements with GCF, which include technical guidelines for use of funds.
   - Ensure that procurement is done through open and competitive bidding processes, including the use of OCR where applicable (Section 4.4 and 5.3).

2. **EE: Eligibility and Subsidy**
   - Ensure that all funds are spent in accordance with individual capability assessments and formal agreements with GCF, which include technical guidelines for use of funds.
   - Ensure that procurement is done through open and competitive bidding processes, including the use of OCR where applicable (Section 4.4 and 5.3).

### Selected Risk Factor F: Exposure to foreign exchange risk or of devaluation of currencies

### Risk Category: Financial

| Probability | Impact |
|-------------|--------|
| High        | Low    |

#### Description
Foreign exchange (FX) risks can be currency, interest rate and commodity price risks. These risks are particularly relevant when funds are borrowed in a foreign currency or when the project is implemented in a local currency. FX risk is also born by the potential of devaluation of currencies used to purchase goods and services. This risk can be mitigated through hedging strategies such as forward contracts, swaps and options. The GCF will use its own resources to mitigate these risks.

### Mitigation Measures

1. **EE: Eligibility and Subsidy**
   - Ensure that all funds are spent in accordance with individual capability assessments and formal agreements with GCF, which include technical guidelines for use of funds.
   - Ensure that procurement is done through open and competitive bidding processes, including the use of OCR where applicable (Section 4.4 and 5.3).

2. **EE: Eligibility and Subsidy**
   - Ensure that all funds are spent in accordance with individual capability assessments and formal agreements with GCF, which include technical guidelines for use of funds.
   - Ensure that procurement is done through open and competitive bidding processes, including the use of OCR where applicable (Section 4.4 and 5.3).

This would result in the net exposure of the public sector to the extent and depth of the de-risking relationships analyzed by this risk factor. The implementation depends on the ability of the borrower to repay the loan. This is based on the assessment of the borrower's creditworthiness. The organization of credit allocation control and borrowing practice should manage conditions, and the risk of default. If a lender participating in the Programme has high-risk debt, exposure induced by the Programme may be limited. The Programme will not be affected by the financial condition for the borrowers or sector of a debt. Also, with regards to Component 1, the Programme will not be affected by the financial condition of the borrower. The AAA rating provided by the IDB guarantees supported by Component 1 is also not affected by the risk factor. The Programme is not expected to have an impact on the overall financial stability.

# GCP POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### G.1.1 Because higher Environmental and Social (ES) risks are identified from the Programme Management Unit (PMU), the ES risk assessment will be conducted at the project level by the Project Management Team (PMT). The PMT will follow the standard mitigation measures. Therefore, this transaction was categorized as Category B.

### G.1.2 The Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) process is a systematic process to identify, predict, and evaluate the environmental impact of a proposed project before it is implemented. It includes the following steps:

- Identification of potential impacts
- Prediction of potential impacts
- Evaluation of potential impacts

### Table 8: Assessment of E&S risks and impacts, and mitigation measures

| E&S Risk and Impact | Mitigation Measures |
|----------------------|---------------------|
| **ESR01** | Conduct an ESIA in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards. |
| **ESR02** | Ensure that the project complies with all applicable environmental laws and regulations. |
| **ESR03** | Follow the IFC Performance Standards for labor and working conditions. |
| **ESR04** | Implement a grievance mechanism to address any complaints or issues raised by affected communities. |
| **ESR05** | Limit the use of hazardous materials in accordance with local laws and regulations. |
| **ESR06** | Assess NGO involvement and benefits to local communities. |
| **ESR07** | Establish a grievance mechanism for addressing complaints from affected communities. |

### G.1.3 The ESIA process will be conducted in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards.

### G.1.4 The PMT will ensure that all environmental and social impacts are identified, predicted, and evaluated in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards.

### G.1.5 The PMT will also ensure that all mitigation measures are implemented in accordance with the IFC Performance Standards.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2 | PAGE 19 OF 8

## T7: Women who have barriers to access the financial sector
The lack of access to capital hinders women from accessing financial services. The lack of gender-friendly financial products in LAC is a major barrier for women entrepreneurs. Gender-responsive financing is necessary for women's businesses to grow and thrive.

### T7.1: The participation of Amazon women
Amazon women are biodiversity guardians who rely on pre-existing natural resources for their livelihoods. They face barriers such as lack of access to credit, limited financial literacy, and unequal distribution of revenues and profits. Access to information and technology is limited due to the remoteness of their communities.

### T7.2: The Gender Action Plan
The Gender Action Plan has been designed to contribute to achieving the existing gender goals and objectives of the GCF. The Program will design a platform to promote women's access to gender-friendly financial services. This will include developing a framework for women to transition from the informal sector to the formal sector, among other activities.

### T7.3: The general assessment and action plan
The general assessment and action plan have been developed with IDB staff and relevant gender and social inclusion experts. The Program will be implemented in collaboration with local partners. The implementation of the general assessment and action plan will be supported by the development of a gender-responsive framework.

2. El objetivo es incluir a los países miembros de la iniciativa, en conformidad con las normas y principios establecidos por el Fondo Verde para el Clima (GCF).

Como Nacionales Designados Autoridad, nosotros subrayamos que no estamos prescindiendo de nuestra capacidad para participar en la iniciativa, como se establece en el fondo propuesto. Los países miembros de la iniciativa son los únicos responsables de la implementación y administración del proyecto.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP173

## Proposal title:
The Amazon Recycling Fund: Enhancing private capital by leveraging public resources to promote sustainable waste management and adaptation results in the Amazon.

## Accredited entity:
Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)

## Countries/Territories:
Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, and Suriname

## Proposed/grant agreement type:
Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
The program seeks to capitalize the new private funds to promote sustainable waste management in the Amazon basin, which is a region with high environmental and social impacts. Investments in the businesses that have high potential for replication and scaling up are planned. The program will support Development and Financial markets (DAFM) to ensure the Amazon Fund to significantly increase its capitalization and strengthen the regime’s resilience.

### Potential
The program capitalizes the new private funds to promote sustainable waste management in the Amazon basin, which is a region with high environmental and social impacts. Investments in the businesses that have high potential for replication and scaling up are planned. The program will support Development and Financial markets (DAFM) to ensure the Amazon Fund to significantly increase its capitalization and strengthen the regime’s resilience.

### Risks
The program capitalizes the new private funds to promote sustainable waste management in the Amazon basin, which is a region with high environmental and social impacts. Investments in the businesses that have high potential for replication and scaling up are planned. The program will support Development and Financial markets (DAFM) to ensure the Amazon Fund to significantly increase its capitalization and strengthen the regime’s resilience.

### Market demand
Market demand data demonstrates the potential demand of the local supply and services, which is expected to be met by the proposed project. The adaptive capacity at the country and regional levels will be enhanced.

# II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

## 2.1 Programme background

The programme will focus on the Amazon region, especially in six countries: Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname. The Amazon region is one of the world's largest tropical rainforests, covering an area of about 5.5 million km², of which 80 per cent is located in Brazil and 13 per cent in nine other countries. The Amazon basin has a population of approximately 25 million people, with 60 per cent living in urban areas. The Amazon region is highly biodiverse, containing about one-third of the world's known species. It is also home to more than 390 indigenous peoples and 1,200 languages.

When it remains moist, the Amazon basin stores a large amount of carbon dioxide (CO₂) and releases oxygen into the atmosphere. The region is also an important source of water for the planet, with 20 per cent of global river discharge flowing through the Amazon basin. The region's climate is highly variable, ranging from tropical rainforests to savannas and semi-arid areas.

The programme will support efforts to maintain the integrity of the Amazon rainforest and the services it provides, including carbon sequestration and storage, water regulation and purification, and biodiversity conservation. It will also support efforts to reduce deforestation and degradation in the region, which is a major source of greenhouse gas emissions. The programme will also support efforts to improve the livelihoods of indigenous peoples and local communities living in the Amazon region.

The programme will be implemented through a combination of direct investments and technical assistance. It will focus on supporting the development of sustainable land use practices, including agroforestry and sustainable forest management. It will also support efforts to improve the governance of the Amazon region, including the development of policies and regulations that promote sustainable development. The programme will also support efforts to strengthen the capacity of local communities and indigenous peoples to manage their natural resources sustainably.

The programme will be implemented in close collaboration with the governments of the six countries, as well as with civil society organizations, indigenous peoples' organizations and other stakeholders. It will be aligned with the priorities of these countries and will be designed to complement and reinforce existing efforts to protect the Amazon region.

# Green Climate Fund

with high levels of flexibility and efficiency for each country, these means that different financing structures can be offered to meet the needs of different market segments. The aim is to ensure that GCF resources are used effectively and efficiently in order to achieve the objectives of the GCF. This includes ensuring that the GCF does not become a funder of business-as-usual activities but rather supports transformational investments that contribute to a low-emission and climate-resilient future.

## NDB with limited discretion to allocate funding

They can use their own beneficiaries directly or through FIs, as per their charter mandate and subject to the requirements of the NDB. The NDB is not a direct funder but rather acts as an intermediary between the GCF and the implementing entities. This means that the NDB will have limited discretion in terms of how it allocates resources.

## When second tier operations are applicable and when first tier FIs are needed

When second tier operations are applicable, the NDB can use its own resources to fund these operations. However, if the NDB is not able to provide sufficient funding for a particular project or program, then the GCF may need to consider providing additional funding through the NDB.

# Bio trainer a chosen operating system for integrators that provide quality, reliable or other resources to companies in their supply chains.

Bio trainer is a chosen operating system for integrators that provide quality, reliable or other resources to companies in their supply chains.

## Subsection 1.1 Entity treatments (GCF ISDR 5.6 million) and intermediate grains

GCF ISDR 4 subprograms have two subcomponents: 12.4 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 2 million) and 12.5 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 3 million). Subcomponent 12.4 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 2 million) has two subcomponents: 12.4.1 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 1.6 million) and 12.4.2 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 0.4 million). Subcomponent 12.5 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 3 million) has two subcomponents: 12.5.1 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 2.4 million) and 12.5.2 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 0.6 million).

### Subsection 1.1.1 Technical cooperation (GCF ISDR 3.6 million)

Intermediate grains are provided to enhance resources for sustainable growth under subcomponent 12.4.1 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 1.6 million). Supervised costs are included in the technical cooperation component (1) support to potential beneficiaries and (2) administrative and operational support and overhead.

### Subsection 1.1.2 Lab and equipment (GCF ISDR 0.8 million)

Subcomponent 12.4 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 2 million) will take the lead to support the following ongoing initiatives: (1) Ammonia 4.0, Bio-fertilizer for sustainable agriculture; (2) Sustainable Agriculture with the support of WFP, the Partnership Initiative on Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience in Africa; and (3) the Green Climate Fund, which is supported by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and designed by Rainforest Alliance. The National Capital Lab will be responsible for implementing these initiatives.

These initiatives are implemented by the government of India with the support of the World Bank. Subcomponent 12.4 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 2 million) and subcomponent 12.5 Intermediate grains (GCF ISDR 3 million) will be implemented in India.

# The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

## The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

### The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

#### The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

##### The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

###### The prevention and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological

The preservation and enhanced management of the biodiversity, both at ecological
levels of the natural forests (which significantly benefits coastal to lowland
forest ecosystems) and the enhancement of the livelihoods of local communities
through sustainable use of forest resources. This includes the promotion of
recreational opportunities, raw materials (food, fiber, medicinal plants,
wood, etc.), and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs), while we put emphasis
on traditional community-based resource management and conservation.

The government's goal on benefits is measured in terms of the direct (15%) and
indirect (85%) contributions to the national economy. The direct contribution
includes the value added by the forestry sector, including employment and other
services, including existing and newly created jobs). This includes (given the
importance of biodiversity) the benefits from the use of genetic resources,
including those that are used in the pharmaceutical industry. The indirect
contribution is estimated to be about 52% of the total value added by the forestry
sector. The government's goal on benefits also includes the generation of direct and
indirect employment, which is estimated at around 17 per cent of total employment.

Other indicators are used to measure the progress towards such as the
increase in the number of protected areas, the increase in the number of species
that have been reintroduced into the wild, the increase in the number of
endangered species that have been saved from extinction, and the increase in the
number of people who have been trained to use traditional knowledge.

In relation to gender issues, the national goal is directed towards (a) the
increase in the participation of women in forest management activities; (b)
the increase in the number of women who are involved in the conservation of
biodiversity and the sustainable use of natural resources; and (c) the
increase in the number of women who have been trained to use traditional knowledge.

The participation of women in forest management activities is estimated at 30%,
with a dedicated budget of US$1.5 million. This includes the provision of training
for women in forest management, including the development and implementation of
strategies for women's empowerment and the inclusion of gender issues in forestry
plans.

Technical training on financial services tailored to women (including micro-
credit initiatives).

Increase in the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels,
including local, national and international levels.

Educating women on their productive factors such as land, animals and capital;
including special criteria for the use of proceeds obtained from the income of
women's groups.

Increased participation of women in the development and implementation of
strategies to combat desertification.

Better promotion and marketing strategies for forest products to boost national and
international markets, coupled with production practices that are environmentally
sound.

The government has also developed a strategy to increase the participation of all
levels of society in biodiversity conservation.

# Climate, Biodiversity, and Biosecurity-related Legal Frameworks

Global efforts to address climate change have led to the development of various legal frameworks that aim to mitigate its impacts. These frameworks include international agreements such as the Paris Agreement, which focuses on reducing greenhouse gas emissions and promoting sustainable practices. Additionally, regional and national laws are implemented to support these global initiatives.

## County-Level Implementation

County-level implementation is crucial for achieving climate goals. Local governments play a significant role in implementing policies that align with national objectives. This includes developing and enforcing regulations related to waste management, renewable energy adoption, and sustainable transportation. At the county level, collaboration with state and federal agencies is essential to ensure effective implementation.

### National Implementation

National-level implementation involves integrating climate change mitigation strategies into existing frameworks. This includes updating laws and policies to reflect current scientific understanding of climate change impacts. Additionally, national governments can provide financial support and technical assistance to counties for implementing local initiatives.

# AI Roundtable on Green Bond Transparency Framework

## The AI Roundtable on Green Bond Transparency Framework (GBTF)

The AI Roundtable on Green Bond Transparency Framework (GBTF) was established in December 2019 to address the lack of transparency and consistency in green bond disclosures. The GBTF is a voluntary initiative that aims to enhance the credibility, integrity, and comparability of green bonds by providing a set of principles and recommendations for disclosure.

### The Green Bond Principles (GBP)

The Green Bond Principles (GBP) were first published in 2014 and have since been updated several times. They provide voluntary process guidelines that recommend how issuers should manage proceeds from green bond issuance, including the use of funds, the management of proceeds, and reporting on the impact of the project.

### The Green Bond Standards (GBS)

The Green Bond Standards (GBS) were developed by the International Capital Market Association (ICMA) in 2017. They provide a set of minimum standards for green bond issuance, including eligibility criteria for projects, process guidelines, and reporting requirements.

## Assessment of Consistency with GBP and GBS

### Environmental and Social Safeguards

#### Programme Background

The programme aims to increase private investment into environmental and social (E&S) risk categories. The Green Bond Standards (GBS) provide a framework for assessing the consistency of green bond issuance with E&S safeguards.

#### E&S Risk Categories and Safeguard Instrument

The GBS sets out four E&S risk categories: low, medium, high, and significant. Each category has specific eligibility criteria and safeguard instruments to mitigate potential adverse impacts. The programme aims to align the GBS with the Equator Principles (EP) and other international standards.

#### Assessment of Consistency with EP

The programme has developed a methodology for assessing the consistency of green bond issuance with the Equator Principles (EP). This methodology is based on the alignment of the GBS with the EP, including the use of common terminology and definitions.

### ESG Assessment and Management of E&S Risks and Impacts

The programme has developed a framework for assessing and managing environmental and social risks and impacts. The framework includes guidelines for conducting ESG assessments, setting ESG targets, and reporting on progress towards achieving these targets.

# The ESMF has been established in accordance with the AE's environmental and social impact framework where finance intermediaries will manage the E&S risks and support in achieving the AE's environmental and social objectives. The ESMF will be developed on a risk-based financial intermediary and approved the AE-specific sub-projects and activities. The ESMF will be managed by the AE and its implementation will be overseen by the management committee.

## General principles
The ESMF will be governed by general principles of environmental and social impact, agriculture, and forestry management. These principles include but are not limited to: promoting sustainable development, protecting biodiversity and natural resources, and ensuring that the environment is protected from degradation. The ESMF will also promote the conservation of natural resources and the protection of the environment.

## ESMF - Labour and working conditions
This programme has been prepared for small-scale farmers and local communities in order to support their livelihoods. It will be implemented through a combination of technical assistance, capacity building, and community-based approaches. The programme will also promote the development of sustainable agricultural practices and the use of environmentally friendly technologies.

## ESMF - Business efficiency and pollution prevention
The objective is to increase the efficiency of businesses by reducing waste and pollution. This can be achieved through the implementation of energy-efficient technologies and processes, as well as the adoption of best practices in waste management. The programme will also promote the use of renewable energy sources and the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.

## ESMF - Land governance and property rights
This programme aims to improve land governance and property rights for small-scale farmers and local communities. This includes promoting secure tenure, improving access to land, and supporting sustainable land management practices. The programme will also promote the development of community-based natural resource management systems.

## ESMF - Sustainable forestry management
The programme aims to promote sustainable forestry management by reducing deforestation and degradation of forests. This can be achieved through the implementation of sustainable forestry practices, such as reduced impact logging, selective logging, and agroforestry. The programme will also support the development of community-based forest management systems.

## ESMF - Sustainable water management
The programme aims to promote sustainable water management by improving access to clean water and sanitation for small-scale farmers and local communities. This can be achieved through the implementation of water-efficient technologies, such as drip irrigation and rainwater harvesting. The programme will also support the development of community-based water management systems.

## ESMF - Sustainable agriculture
The programme aims to promote sustainable agriculture by supporting the adoption of environmentally friendly farming practices. This includes promoting the use of organic fertilizers, reducing the use of chemical pesticides, and improving soil health. The programme will also support the development of community-based agricultural cooperatives.

## Gender policy

The gender assessment provides the context of the existing environment in the on and off-campus settings for women students at the University of the Western Cape (UWC). The UWC Gender Equity Policy is based on the principles enshrined in the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, the United Nations Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women and the general principles of human rights. It also takes cognisance of the United Nations Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women. The conditions of the respective categories and locations that have been identified are based on the gender assessment findings.

Each of these issues has been established women's concerns with recommendations for addressing them. The University has set up a Gender Equity Unit (GEU) to address these issues. The GEU has set up a Gender Equity Committee (GEC) which is responsible for policy and advocacy matters, the establishment of policies and procedures, the monitoring of gender equity programmes and the implementation of the UWC Gender Policy (GP) and policy with a focus on discrimination violence in the case of Gender Based Violence (GBV). Despite these efforts, there are still issues that need to be addressed. The University has identified resources and opportunities which can be used to address these issues.

The gender assessment was conducted based on a desk review and it is expected that the recommendations will be implemented as part of the overall strategy for addressing gender equity concerns at UWC. The Gender Equity Unit (GEU) has been established to coordinate and implement the Gender Policy (GP). The GEU has also been responsible for the implementation of the Gender Equity Committee (GEC) which is responsible for policy and advocacy matters, the establishment of policies and procedures, the monitoring of gender equity programmes and the implementation of the UWC Gender Policy (GP).

# Green Climate Fund

## Introduction

They do not experience climate change over land widely; they do play an essential role in structural mechanisms and local benefits of environmental conservation practices. Women are more vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their dependence on natural resources for subsistence, including impacts for compounds on reproductive systems of agriculture, livestock, and fisheries. They also face increased risks from disasters and other environmental threats. The impact of climate change on women's livelihoods is exacerbated by gender inequalities in access to resources and services that may bring in such resources. It has tremendous effects on their livelihoods as they are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes that shape their lives, prevent them from participating fully in environmental management opportunities. Women's participation in environmental management is essential for the sustainability of natural resource management and the achievement of gender equality targets. It is also important to ensure that women have access to information and resources needed to participate effectively in environmental management.

## The All Plan

The All plan provides a general action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Program. The program will be implemented through a series of challenges and opportunities for women and children households, individuals, targets, timelines, and budgets. As part of the program, the GCF will provide support to the implementation of the program and its objectives.

The program is designed to address the needs of women and children in the community. The general objective is to ensure that all women and children have access to the resources they need to participate effectively in environmental management. The program will be implemented through a series of challenges and opportunities for women and children households, individuals, targets, timelines, and budgets. As part of the program, the GCF will provide support to the implementation of the program and its objectives.

The general action plan includes creating opportunities, including credibility and transparency in environmental management systems. Women, youth, and indigenous peoples are included in the development of the program. The program is designed to ensure that all women and children have access to the resources they need to participate effectively in environmental management. The program will be implemented through a series of challenges and opportunities for women and children households, individuals, targets, timelines, and budgets. As part of the program, the GCF will provide support to the implementation of the program and its objectives.

# Green Climate Fund

## Colombia, Guatemala, Guyana, Peru and Suriname: The AF is providing co-financing of up to USD 15 million, under loan, and up to USD 30 million in grants, to USD 45 million in total.

For countries GCP will provide 100,136 beneficiaries of solar home systems for off-grid communities. For the remaining countries, the AF will provide co-financing of up to USD 20 million, under loan, and up to USD 10 million in grants, for a total of USD 30 million.

### Accredited entities/accelerating entity capability in respect of the current programme (not including head)

The AF has three track records with the GCF. The first was the accreditation of the AF as an implementing entity and the second was the approval of the first loan to the AF. The third was the approval of the first grant facility funding the AF. The EE shall hold its funds from the GCF in a separate account, which is subject to the control of the EE. The EE shall provide support (early stage) to businesses USD 4 million renewable grants and USD 5 million in co-financing under the AF.

Greenhouse gas emissions reductions or climate change adaptation benefits will be achieved through the following activities:

- Energy efficient appliances
- Renewable energy mini grids
- Energy efficiency in public buildings and street lighting
- Energy efficient transport
- Energy efficient industrial processes
- Energy efficient agriculture
- Energy efficient residential housing

# Green Credit Card

## Issued with a government guarantee

Therefore, for USD 135 million in loans, GCF will be exposed to credit risk (its own counterparty). The level of credit (its own counterparty) is different from the counter party in the case of IBRD and IDA. The credit rating of IBRD and IDA by Fitch and Standard & Poor's is AA+ and A+, respectively, while that of GCF is not yet rated. However, the Board has approved credit risk management for these commitments. The level of counterparty (IDB and the Fund) and the credit risk are managed by the Fund. The exposure to credit risk is limited to USD 135 million and will be increased in the financial period, contingent on approval of the IBRD second and third loans. This is an argument for the alignment of GCF with the IDB. Allowance for a high country of ownership means it sets up an additional layer of protection against default by the counterparty. The Fund has no recourse to the assets of the counterparty.

## Pre-issuance costs

Pre-issuance costs are estimated at USD 0.5 million (an expense) and highly correlated with the credit risk of the counterparty. These expenses include legal fees, due diligence, and other pre-issuance costs related to the issuance. The requirement of pre-issuance grants (up to USD 14 is initially provided for in the budget) is justified by the need to ensure that the counterparty is creditworthy. The counterparty risk is expected to be high because the guarantee will cover risk for the counterparty and the Fund. The counterparty risk is limited to USD 0.5 million (an expense). The pre-issuance costs are capped at 30% of the underlying loan amount.

## Currency risk

Currency risk is estimated at USD 14 million (an expense) and highly correlated with the credit risk of the counterparty. The currency risk is limited to the amount of the underlying loan amount. The currency risk is expected to be high because the guarantee will cover risk for the counterparty and the Fund. The currency risk is limited to USD 14 million (an expense). The pre-issuance costs are capped at 30% of the underlying loan amount.

## Interest rate risk

Interest rate risk is estimated at USD 28 million (an expense) and highly correlated with the credit risk of the counterparty. The interest rate risk is limited to the amount of the underlying loan amount. The interest rate risk is expected to be high because the guarantee will cover risk for the counterparty and the Fund. The interest rate risk is limited to USD 28 million (an expense). The pre-issuance costs are capped at 30% of the underlying loan amount.

## Default risk

The pre-issuance will focus on existing conditions to increase the flow of business to the borrowing. Lenders denied for credit financing or lack of resources will be excluded from this program. The default risk is expected to be high because the guarantee will cover risk for the counterparty and the Fund. The default risk is limited to USD 135 million (an expense). The pre-issuance costs are capped at 30% of the underlying loan amount.

## Foreign exchange risk

GCF lending will consider counterparties with a USD currency exposure. Therefore, GCF will be exposed to foreign exchange risk. The ability to repay potentially reduces the need for insurance against this risk. The counterparty risk is expected to be high because the guarantee will cover risk for the counterparty and the Fund. The default risk is limited to USD 135 million (an expense). The pre-issuance costs are capped at 30% of the underlying loan amount.

## Carbon credit

Although the sale of carbon credits is not an objective of the project, it is expected that the sale of carbon credits will be a significant source of revenue. The carbon credits generated by GCP will be sold to the highest bidder. The carbon credits are expected to be sold at market prices and the carbon credits are the subject of a condition of the project.

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

### WWI
### Capacity Constraints and Reinforcement Abilities

The AL has performed the capacity assessment on the EUs (NNBs), and has determined a need for enhanced cooperation with the EU to ensure the safety and security of these facilities. Taking into consideration the identified challenges associated with the reinforcement abilities, it is recommended that the AL enhance its efforts to reinforce the capacities of the NNBs. This will enable the AL to effectively address any potential risks and vulnerabilities in the future.

The AL has also performed a risk assessment with selected cooperation and coordination, and there was no flagged assessment with this program. As a result, the AL has identified several areas for improvement. These include enhancing the safety and security of the NNBs, improving the effectiveness of the AL's internal monitoring and oversight mechanisms, and strengthening the AL's capacity to respond to any potential risks.

### GFC portfolio construction risk (low risk)

In case of agreement, the impact of this proposal on the GFC portfolio risk remains within the risk appetite.

### Protect credibility and consistency

The AL should continue to promote the development of nuclear safety and security culture and practices. In this regard, the AL could also provide the details of financing terms for the EU program. Before that, the AL should ensure that the AL's internal monitoring and oversight capacity is adequate to protect the integrity of GFC Reinforcement funds with the final beneficiaries.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP173

## Proposal name:
The Amazon Bioconomy Fund (unlocking private capital by valuing bioconomy products and services with climate impact)

## Accredited entity:
Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)

## Countries(ies):
Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname

## Proposed programme size:
Large

## L. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential
**Scale:** Medium to high

**Concept:** The Amazon Bioconomy Fund is a large-scale programmatic funding proposal submitted by the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB). The program aims to unlock private capital for bioconomy products and services with climate impact in the Amazon region. The fund will provide technical assistance to support the preparation of the projects, furthering the implementation of the Paris Agreement on Climate Change. The fund seeks to leverage private sector investments for sustainable development and self-sufficiency in the Amazon region. It aims to unlock private capital by valuing bioconomy products and services with climate impact.

**Low-emission and climate change resilience benefits:** The Amazon Bioconomy Fund will support businesses in the target countries to develop and implement low-carbon technologies, practices, and business models that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and enhance climate resilience. The fund will also promote sustainable land use and forest management, which can help mitigate climate change.

**Additional benefits:** The fund will provide technical assistance to support the development of bioconomy products and services with climate impact in the Amazon region. This will help to create jobs and stimulate economic growth in the target countries. The fund will also promote the adoption of sustainable practices by businesses and governments, which can help to protect biodiversity and ecosystems.

**Potential risks:** The main potential risk is that the fund may not be able to unlock sufficient private capital to achieve its objectives. Another risk is that the technical assistance provided by the fund may not be effective in helping businesses to develop and implement low-carbon technologies and practices.

# Development of green employment in the region

The programme is strengthening regional integration among Amazon countries, building common funds by supporting the development of a regional market for environmental services and products. Appropriate and systematic approach will increase productivity and green employment in the region.

## The proposed programme considers a set of interlinked individual projects

### Promotion of green jobs and entrepreneurship

Promotion of green jobs and entrepreneurship in the Amazon region, through the reinforcement of the relevance of targeted value chains and economies in the Amazon region by capitalizing on the comparative advantages of the countries. This will include the promotion of micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) and the development of a regional market for environmental services and products.

## Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: High

1. As mentioned in paragraph 1 of this assessment, the programme contributes to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 8 on decent work and economic growth, by promoting sustainable development and with the help of its partnerships. The fact that the programme is significant for the Amazon region is also highlighted by the fact that it contributes to the sustainable development that is central to the UNFCCC RBIs. change (R&D00) (deforestation and forest degradation), and the Lettia Park (sustainable development of the region's bio-resources capacity). The analysis demonstrates that climate change is a major threat to biodiversity and ecosystem services in the region. It also highlights the need for integrated approaches to address these challenges effectively.

Including protected GCF funds in this investment initiative will allow governments to use their resources more efficiently, particularly in developing countries where financial and technical assistance mechanisms such as sustainability and climate resilience are limited. The integration of these funds into regional TAP initiatives should be the starting point. These initiatives can be expanded by integrating them with other programs that promote sustainable development and climate change mitigation. This will ensure that the benefits of GCF funding are maximized, particularly in developing countries where climate change is a major threat to biodiversity.

# Needs of the recipient

## i.

Governments are innovatively applying and providing the AE to more than 34,000 users in 16 countries. The AE is used by governments to support their own development efforts and to improve the effectiveness of their own integrated and inter-sectoral planning processes ongoing in Amazonian countries such as Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, Suriname, Venezuela, and Bolivia. The governments of these countries have also established the Regional Amazon Coordination Office (ORPA) which is an initiative of seven Amazon countries concluded as the Loreto Pact are provided before.

## ii.

Goals and needs identified by the AE during the survey:

### a.

The AE has been used to support the development of a multi-sectoral planning system for REDD plus activities in Peru. The government of Peru has also developed a national strategy for the management of carbon, specifically community forest management. The funding mechanism for this is through the sale of carbon credits generated by the project. The AE confirms that all REDD plus activities will be reported to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

### b.

Limited technical and financial capacities were also identified as hindering the effective use of the AE in several countries. Although governments are interested in training officials and government personnel, there is a lack of resources for this purpose. The AE has been used to support the development of these projects.

### c.

Several countries have not yet adopted their second national and policy frameworks with respect to REDD plus activities. This means that the AE has been used to support the development of national strategies and policies in several countries. The AE is also being used to support the development of national strategies for the implementation of REDD plus activities in several countries.

### d.

No participant country has a full system of REDD plus activities in place.

## iii.

Training:

The AE has been used to train government officials on how to use the AE and how to develop their own national strategies. The AE is also being used to support the development of national strategies for the implementation of REDD plus activities in several countries.

# Biodiversity and deforestation remain a major issue in all countries in the region.

## The majority of countries do not have dedicated high-level frameworks governing the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity.

### For example, Colombia has adopted a special law on biodiversity, but it does not include a national biodiversity plan and has a limited budget for institutional biodiversity research. Brazil has a national biodiversity strategy and action plans, but these are not integrated into the country's development strategies.

## The 2016-2022 Strategic Plan for Biodiversity and the Aichi Biodiversity Targets provide an opportunity to enhance integration of biodiversity conservation and sustainable use into national development planning.

### Brazil has developed a National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plans, which are integrated with other sectoral plans. However, most countries do not have comprehensive plans for sustainable agriculture beyond the national level.

## Policies to foster the development of sustainable agriculture and forestry are beginning to emerge, but there is a priority for more effective implementation and enforcement.

### The subregional TIP considers that the needs of the recipient country are very high and that the GEF program could contribute significantly to addressing these needs. Some of the major threats to biodiversity in the region include deforestation, habitat fragmentation, pollution, over-exploitation of natural resources, and climate change.

# Brazil, Colombia and Peru have adopted specific legal frameworks governing climate change mitigation and adaptation. Brazil and Peru also have climate-related frameworks.

## Five countries have produced specific guidelines related to climate change

### Brazil

The Brazilian government has developed a National Climate Change Policy (PNMC) that aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change in the environment, energy, agriculture, and transportation sectors. The PNMC is aligned with the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals.

### Colombia

Colombia has adopted a general policy framework that covers some areas of reference to the biosafety law, such as protocols for export services, environmental management, sustainable fisheries, ecosystem services, and technological research. Among the main objectives are the conservation of biodiversity and the development of renewable energy sources. The Colombian government has also developed a National Climate Change Policy (PNACC) that aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change.

### Peru

Subnational governments in Brazil and Peru have also adopted biosafety frameworks, which include protocols for export services, environmental management, sustainable fisheries, ecosystem services, and technological research. Among the main objectives are the conservation of biodiversity and the development of renewable energy sources. The Peruvian government has also developed a National Climate Change Policy (PNACC) that aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change.

### Other countries

Other countries have adopted frameworks that address both mitigation and adaptation, such as the REDD+ plan, which includes biodiversity conservation, sustainable forest management, and ecosystem services. These frameworks are designed to promote sustainable development and are often focused on specific products, with national laws coordinating with international agreements.

### Payments for ecosystem services (PES)

Payments for ecosystem services (PES) are a key component of the REDD+ plan in Brazil, Colombia, Peru, and other countries. PES is an incentive mechanism that rewards landowners for conservation activities that maintain or enhance ecosystem services. PES can be used to promote sustainable development and are often focused on specific products, with national laws coordinating with international agreements.

### Agroecology projects

Agroecology projects have been developed in Brazil, Colombia, Peru, and other countries. These projects include the use of organic farming methods, sustainable agriculture practices, and the promotion of biodiversity. The agroecology projects are designed to promote sustainable development and are often focused on specific products, with national laws coordinating with international agreements.

### The content of the funding proposal will be in accordance with the legal text in compliance with REDD+ plan activities and what all countries have adopted from the Paris Agreement.

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund: Annex 8 Gender Assessment

## 1. INTRODUCTION

More than 34 million people, including 420 indigenous peoples, live in the Amazon Region (Panamazonica or Great Amazon) and depend on nature for food security, livelihoods, and well-being. The region is home to 59% of Brazil's indigenous population, with 187 different ethnic groups, of which half have less than 200 individuals each. Half live in urban centers. The most populous cities are Manaus and Belém in Brazil, with about 2 million inhabitants each.

This six-countries deal is included in the agenda: The Amazon Bioconomy Fund - Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, and Suriname. These countries have a common interest in education, employment, entrepreneurship, social participation, access to finance, and the promotion of sustainable development. They also share concerns about cultural and structural barriers in these countries, which are worsened in rural areas and among indigenous peoples.

The Amazon Fund is located in the Amazon. As users of forest products and guardians of traditional knowledge, women have always been involved in the Amazonian economy. Nevertheless, their access to forest resources and the economic benefits generated by them has been limited due to gender inequalities.

On the other hand, the emergence of COVID-19 has revealed the fundamental role that women play at the first line of engagement and their role as caregivers at home that has increased their workload. This situation has worsened because: i) women are more present in the sectors most affected by the coronavirus pandemic; ii) they are more involved in the care of children, elderly people, and sick relatives; iii) they have been disproportionately affected by the economic crisis caused by the pandemic; iv) they are the main informal sector or take jobs with lower salaries. About 12 million women LAC lost their jobs.

## 2. GENDER CONTEXT

Legal regularity and institutional frameworks. The six countries have ratified or acceded to the Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belém do Pará), and the Convention on the Rights of the Child.

The data shows a lack of gender equality in the region. According to the World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report 2021, the average score for Latin America is 73.4 points out of 100. The countries with the lowest scores are: Bolivia (68.9), Peru (69.5), and Colombia (70.1). The highest scores are in Uruguay (83.3) and Chile (82.8).

CEDAW is an international treaty adopted in 1979 by the United Nations General Assembly. Developed as a human rights instrument, it aims to eliminate discrimination against women.

### A. BRAZIL

The 1988 Constitution symbolizes the legal framework for the re-democratization of Brazil. For the formulation of the 1988 Constitution, popular participation was ensured. With regard to women's participation, the Charter of Brazilian Women to be elected as a member of the Constitutional Assembly was created.

The 1988 Constitution states on, also involving by popularly its text bringing fundamental rights, and already in Article 5 establishes equality between men and women, guaranteeing the right to life, liberty, security, and citizenship. The Civil Code of 1916 contained several provisions that placed women in an inferior role compared to men. However, the 1988 Constitution changed this scenario and that the rights and duties regarding to be conjugal society are treated equally by men and women and that the State is an equal assistance to the family in the exercise of their responsibilities. The 1988 text was consented with expressly reproducing the protection of women's rights.

### Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights

Ministry of Human Rights (2015-2016) and Secretary for Human Rights of the Presidency of the Republic (1993-2014), and the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights, created in 2015, guarantees the promotion, protection, and defense human rights, children, adolescents, elderly women, and people with disabilities.

### National Secretary of Policies for Women (SNPM)

Linked to the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights, it combines all forms of violence and discrimination against women. The SNPM is responsible for implementing public policies aimed at combating domestic and family violence, trafficking in persons, sexual exploitation, and other crimes. The National Secretary of Policies for Women is linked to the organization, structure of the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights.

The Convention of Belem do Para is an international human rights instrument adopted by the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights (IACHR) on June 9, 1994. It is the first legally binding international treaty that condemns all forms of violence against women.

### Law No. 13.104/2015 ("Lei da Femicidio" (2015))

On March 9, 2015, Brazil's existing criminal code was amended to criminalize femicide, with sentencing guidelines that distinguish between femicide and other forms of homicide.

### Law N° 13.911/2019 "Forced and Coerced Sterilization"

This law amends Article 1320 of the Civil Code in order to establish that only persons who have given their consent may be sterilized. The Civil Code may vary this rule as long as it does not affect the principle of free will.

### The Maria da Penha Law

Law No. 11.340/2006 amended the Brazilian Penal Code to include domestic violence against women in every crime of physical, psychological, sexual, patrimonial, moral or social violence in terms of national legislation on gender.

## B. COLOMBIA

The 1991 Constitution establishes (i) the principle of non-discrimination for reasons of sex, race, nationality or family origin; religious, political or union rights (article 13); and (ii) the right to equality before the law and to equal protection by public authorities in the exercise of their functions. It also recognizes the right to protection against all forms of violence within the family that is considered destructive of the harmony and stability of the family unit, and establishes the obligation of the State to protect women and children from domestic violence (article 42).

The Presidential Council for the Equality of Women, dependent on the Vice Presidency of the Republic, was created in 1998 with the aim of designing policies, plans, programs and necessary provisions designed to eliminate discrimination against women. It also has the function of promoting agreements and accords that relate to gender equality for women and (ii) assessing the impact of public policies on women's organizations, international organizations, organizations from civil society and other actors in the formulation and implementation of public policy.

In the last three decades, national governments have developed different policies for the promotion of gender equality. In 1993, the Colombian Congress approved Law 256/1996, the Plan for Equal Opportunities for Women; in 2003, the Women Peace Builder Policy and in 2013

## C. EQUADOR

The 2008 Constitution acquires a series of commitments to achieve gender equality, including promotion of parity in the appointment of public positions, elimination of discrimination based on sex or sexual preference, and the right of persons with disabilities to achieve equality of men and women, guarantee of women's access to justice, protection against all forms of violence, equality in access to employment, recognition as productive work of labor-replaced self-sustaining and family care tasks, price, homes, assistance for single mothers and their children, and the right to a healthy environment.

As of the approval of the 2008 Constitution, the different public policies generated by the State are oriented towards the effective exercise of rights to achieve equality between women and men. The Ministry of Women and Gender Equality is responsible for the coordination and technical assistance for a mandatory application in the public sector.

The National Council for Gender Equality was created in 1975 as the Supreme Council of Women, Law in 1983 becomes the National Office for Women in 1984 is the Ministry of Social Welfare. The Ministry of Social Welfare promotes the full equality of women in political, legal, psychological, economic, educational and social aspects.

In becomes the National Council of Women CONAMU with administrative, financial and technical autonomy, which is part of the structure of the Ministry of Social Inclusion. CONAMU provides advice and assistance to the Ministry for a mandatory application in a public policy that ensures the validity and exercise of the rights and duties of women and men.

The National Apenda for Women and LGBT people 2016-2037 is a mandatory instrument of national planning and is aligned with the national Development Plan 2017-2021. It includes measures to promote gender equality and due to Gender, both for public policies and for the private sector.

### Overview of Rights Holders and Legal Framework

#### Organic Code of the Judicial Function
- Ensures the independence of judges.
- Establishes the rights of victims of violence against women and the family.

#### Organic Law on Violence Against Women and Family
- Protects women in cases of domestic violence, sexual abuse, and economic exploitation.
- Establishes penalties for perpetrators.

#### Organic Law on Political Participation
- Ensures equal rights to participate in political life.
- Promotes gender equality in politics.

#### Organic Law of the Legislative Function
- Incorporates the focus on women's rights and gender equality.
- Creates the norm and provides the Specialized Commission for Gender Equality.
- Proceeds with creating the norm and provides the Specialized Commission for Gender Equality, taking into account the impact on indigenous peoples, as well as the use of non-discriminatory language in the standard and the gender impact of the suggested norms.

#### Organic Law of the Judicial Function
- Ensures the independence of judges.
- Establishes the rights of victims of violence against women and the family.

#### Organic Code of Criminal Procedure
- Ensures due process for all citizens, including equal treatment before the law.

#### Organic Code of Criminal Procedure
- Ensures due process for all citizens, including equal treatment before the law.

#### Organic Law on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities
- Guarantees citizens, individually or collectively, equal rights and opportunities in political, economic, social, cultural, and public life.
- Establishes the State and society's responsibility to protect these rights.

#### Organic Code of Planning and Public Finance
- Ensures that consultation spaces will be established in the event of planning and public policy, in order to promote participation, gender equality, diversity, and inclusion.

#### The Comprehensive Organic Code on Criminal Procedure and Admissibility of Evidence
- Typifies three types of crimes: violence against women or family members, psychological abuse, and sexual violence.
- Includes crimes such as sexual exploitation, trafficking, and loss of children's rights.
- Establishes the rights of victims to be heard in court.

#### Work Code
- Specifies the rights of working women and men, particularly those in vulnerable situations.
- Ensures equal pay for equal work.
- Establishes the right to health insurance and medical care.

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

### Law on Violence against Women
It makes an explicit approach to violence against women and the family. It includes protection measures for victims of domestic violence. Article 1 provides that physical and mental integrity is a fundamental right of every woman and member of the family.

### Free Maternity and Child Care Law
It is the right of women to free quality and safe health care, during pregnancy, childhood and postpartum. It also includes the right to receive adequate nutrition for pregnant women and children under five years old.

### Organic Law of Public Service of 2010
It establishes equal presence of men and women in the nomination and appointment positions, in the selection processes of public servants. It also promotes the participation of women in decision-making bodies.

### Organic Law of Basic Education
Establishes social equality between men and women. Includes: gender equity, non-discrimination, and the right to education for all, in order to achieve a change in discriminatory cultural patterns.

### Organic Law of Higher Education
Establishes the principle of government that is understood as the shared management of universities and higher education institutions. It promotes quality, equal opportunities, alternation, equality and gender equity.

### Organic Law of Communication
Promotes discrimination against reasons of ethnicity, gender, age, social status, sexual orientation, physical past and immigration.

### Organic Law of Popular and Solidarity Economy
It establishes its particular economic units those that are dedicated to the care economy, among other activities. It also promotes the participation of women in decision-making bodies as those who actively carry out activities for the preparation of food, human care and others.

Women’s rights is ensured by according women access with men to education, employment, health care, housing, property ownership, and participation in political, social and cultural activities. Special laws and health protection measures for women by promoting conditions enabling mothers to work, and ensuring equal pay.

## E. PERU

The 1993 Political Constitution of Peru establishes that no one should be discriminated against or receive unequal treatment due to their sex, race, social condition or any other nature.

### The Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations
Is to become an autonomous government agency responsible for promoting the rights of women and vulnerable groups, with a focus on comprehensive care and prevention of gender-based violence.

### The General Directorate for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination
Oversees compliance with national and sectoral policies for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination. It is responsible for ensuring that all public institutions comply with their objectives and the scope of its competence (Supreme Decree No. 003-2012-MIMDES).

### The Gender and Climate Change Action Plan
Is a management instrument that seeks to guide the action of different entities of the Peruvian State so that women can benefit from the development process, with special attention to those most affected by climate change.

### National Equality Framework that Guarantees Actions
Law No. 28093 - Law of Equality and Non-Discrimination

The Peruvian Constitution establishes that all persons are equal before the law; it also adopts the necessary measures to provide and guarantee equality between women and men in the exercise of their rights.

### Supreme Decree No. 027-2015-PCM
Defines and establishes mandatory national policies, (Equality of Opportunities for Women and Men) and establishes the responsibilities of public entities to promote gender equality.

### Policy Framework for Gender Equality
Decree No. 043-2013-MIMDES
Establishes that all public institutions must monitor and contribute to the effective implementation of its strategic objectives and expected results, as well as its indicators.

### Supreme Degree No. 974/2018-MMP
Formulates the use of the National System of Gender Indicators. It considers this system as an instrument for the follow-up monitoring and evaluation of public policies.

### Decree No. 6,027/2012-MMP
Approves the National Gender Equality Policy. Six priority objectives are established in it: Reduce violence against women; Promote equal opportunities between men and women in health; Guarantee the access and participation of women in political life; Promote the economic empowerment of women; Promote the recognition of domestic work as a social right; and reduce institutional barriers that hinder equality in the public and private spheres between men and women. It also establishes the National Gender Equality Council to monitor the implementation of this policy.

### SURNAME

Surname's Constitution of 1987 with Amendments through 1982 states that (i) no person shall be denied equal protection of the laws by any State or any subdivision thereof, nor denied the equal protection of the laws within any State; (ii) all persons are entitled to equal rights under the law regardless of race, color, sex, age, religion, national origin, or economic status. It equates discrimination against women with discrimination based on race and ethnicity. It also establishes that no person shall be denied employment because of age, sex, race, nationality, response to particular opinions, the right to a remunerative job commensurate to their ability, and freedom from coercion and abusive labor practices.

The Bureau for Gender Policy was established in 1987 and became operationally active in 1988. In 2015, the name of the bureau was changed to the Bureau of Gender Equality. The Bureau is responsible for the implementation of the National Gender Policy, with the objective to develop, coordinate, monitor, evaluate and promote gender equality policies and programs. It also aims to ensure equal opportunities for men and women.

Since it was set up, the Bureau has developed two Integrated Gender Plans of Action (IGAP 2000-2005 and IGAP 2006-2010) and installed Gender Focal Points (GFPs) in all ministries. The Bureau also developed a Gender Equality Policy for Rural Areas.

Surname's Multi-Year Development Plan 2010-2019 was led by a rights-based approach to development, with the aim of promoting gender equality in programs, projects and activities. The development of gender policies for rural areas

For example, in Brazil, 81% of women are literate compared to 73% of men; while in Peru 72.7% of women access this type of education compared to 68.1% of men.

Employment: Although women have a higher rate of education than men, the latter face fewer barriers to employment and tend to be more employed. For example, in some countries such as Brazil, women represent 43.9% of the labor force, although these gaps are wider in some countries such as Peru where women represent 72.5% compared to 67.3% of men. COCV-19 pandemic ECLAC estimated that the female participation rate in the labor market decreased by 0.8 percentage points between March and April 2020, while for men it was only 0.4 percentage points. About 12 million women left the regular workforce due to job destitution.

The percentage of unemployment among women is higher in all countries and varies from 7% to 35%. In Colombia, 35% of women are unemployed compared to 11.4% of men; COCV-19 updates indicate that in Peru, 28.6% of women are unemployed compared to 10.4%, while the estimated female unemployment in the LAC region rate decreases to 12.0% in 2020.

The part-time employment has one of the largest gender gaps. All the Amazonian countries present large gaps that show women occupying these jobs. For example, in Colombia, 63.7% of women work part-time compared to 45.8% of men; and in Ecuador 49.1% of women are employed in this type of job.

Women dedicate between 3 and 5.5 hours per day to unpaid work while men dedicate only 2 hours on average, according to the study by ECLAC. In Brazil, women spend an average of 2 hours and 47 minutes per day on unpaid work compared to 1 hour and 18 minutes for men.

Political representation: Women are poorly represented in the parliaments of their countries. They represent between 15% and 36% of parliamentarians.

### 3. AMAZON POPULATION

#### Social groups and division by sex

The Amazon Region has a population of about 34 million people, of which 1.8% are indigenous (IBGE, 2019 RAISG, 2020). There is no clarity on how many “hidden indigenous” live in this region. The term “hidden indigenous” refers to those who do not identify as such but have some indigenous ancestry. According to the Amazonian Georeferenced Socio-Environmental Information Network (RAISG, 2020), there are 354 ethnic groups and 178 languages in voluntary isolation and initial contact (PIACI) there; see the RAISG (2020) report for the existence of our PIACI base map. LTPCA (2019) reports that, as of 2016, there are 54 isolated ethnic groups in voluntary isolation and initial contact (PIACI) in the Amazon region.

> “Hidden indigenous” means people in Brazil. According to the GT Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention, these are people who do not identify themselves as indigenous but have some indigenous ancestry. They live in the same villages or communities, and whose status is registered officially or partially by their own community. The term “hidden indigenous” was coined by anthropologist Carlos Alberto Pereira in 2014 to describe those who inhabit the country, a geographical region, or an ethnic group. This concept includes people of mixed race, indigenous ancestry, and non-indigenous ancestry.

The term “hidden indigenous” refers to those who do not identify as such but have some indigenous ancestry.
