# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

The other communities that live in the region are distributed as follows: 62% peasants, 18% indigenous people, and 10% quimbo and urban (PAHO, 2019). These groups have different gender compositions and 1% identify as LGBTI (PAHO, 2019). For example, in the Ecuadorian Amazon 53.1% of the population is indigenous, while 46.9% are Afro-descendants or mestizos. In other departments such as black, mestizo and indigenous (INEC, 2016). For its part, the Peruvian Amazon has a similar distribution: 57.8% indigenous people and 39.6% quimbo and mestizo; in addition to being a peasant community, mestizo, indigenous population slightly exceeds 23% and peasants who identify as black or indigenous are over 2% (INEI, 2016).

The overview of the Amazonian populations by sex has not been accurately detailed. However, according to the most recent estimates, it is estimated that for five countries in the Amazon region: Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Brazil and Bolivia, the population is predominantly male with an average of 49.7% of the population. This population varies according to the different ethnic groups that inhabit them. For example, in the Peruvian Amazon, 50.3% are men and 49.7% women; while in Ecuador, 51.6% are men and 48.4% women (INEC, 2016). Finally, in Peru 58.8% of the population is male and 41.2% female (INEI, 2017), as well as in Colombia 53.9% are men and 46.1% women (DANE, 2018).

Most of the population in Brazil, according to more than 70% of the total population. Such population is followed by that of Peru and Colombia with an estimated 13% and 4%, respectively. The most important cities are Manaus and Belem in Brazil, Iquitos in Peru, and Leticia in Colombia; with a population of more than 500,000 inhabitants (INEI, 2017). Among the main municipalities in the region are other important cities such as Boa Vista, Puerto Maldonado, Macapa, Manaus, Belem, Iquitos, and Leticia. The population ranges from 80,000 to 500,000 people (INEI, 2017; National University of Colombia, s.f.).

## Gender relations in the Amazon

According to a paper published by CIFOR (CIFOR, 2015) there are several features of gender relations in the Amazon. However, each of these varies greatly depending on the population and their cultural groups.

- Strong patriarchal societies and patrilocality prevail, with women's mobility being limited.
- Gender roles and values among some indigenous groups and among the general population in northeastern Brazil assume that men represent the family and the


---
**Page 162**
---

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

community in public arenas, leaving women without independent autonomous property rights and representation in decision making needed to create sustainable development.

- The division of labor by gender is often linked to different physical spaces and activities. Women are more likely to be involved in subsistence activities in the homestead/community garden, swidden gardens, including planting, weeding, harvesting, processing, and marketing. Men typically dominate in hunting, agricultural clearing and logging activities for the commercial market.
- Women's productive work is often invisible to markets and customers due to its informal nature and lack of recognition by formal institutions.
  - **ACCESS:** Formal status of many forest communities makes women's exclusive access more difficult, and limits access to social services and other benefits of citizenship.
  - **CONTROL:** Women have less control over land and resources than men. This is often due to the informal nature of their work and lack of legal recognition.
  - **BENEFITS:** Programs to support community forest management have influenced community and family gender dynamics, often creating or exacerbating gender inequalities by excluding women from decision making processes in natural resource management (NRM) and broader forest management tasks.
- **PARTICIPATION:** Women's participation in community forestry has been limited. Women have had fewer ways to organize in support of their community struggles for land and other resources, to participate in training and capacity building activities, and to develop skills and leadership within broader community and public arenas.

## WOMEN IN FINANCE IN LAC

### Women and Businesses

There is mounting global evidence of a correlation between gender diversity and superior business results and better investor returns. McKinsey finds a GDP impact growth of $12 trillion if women's participation in the market economy is at a similar level as men. However, gender diversity remains low across most sectors. Only 37% of companies have at least one woman on their board of directors, and only 5% have more than three. In LAC, 63% of AAC companies declare that gender diversity is not a strategic priority.

In the Amazonian countries, the presence of women on boards of directors is very low, with participation between 8.4% in Brazil and 15.1% in Colombia. The figures improve when considering only female CEOs, but still remain below 20%.


---
**Page 163**
---

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

a little in the companies where women are majority owners, although they do not exceed 20% in any of the 15 countries in this study. Although the number of companies will increase to 38 by the end of 2019, the proportion is unlikely to change significantly.

Women represent more than 40% of the economically active population in LAC. Only 17% of them are entrepreneurs in the formal economy. The main barriers they encounter to starting a business include lack of access to credit and financial services, limited access to education, and lack of support from family and friends. The main reasons women give for not working in the sector[^1] or giving up microenterprises, relying on their husbands' income, are:

- The burden (in ignorance) of the financial market and existing opportunities.
- Lack of financial education.
- Lack of family support to follow a business.
- Lack of self-confidence.
- Lack of access to credit.

According to a study of 35 entrepreneurs in 15 countries in LAC, a high number of women entrepreneurs are married (70%) compared with men (48%). A higher percentage of women entrepreneurs have children (69.7% versus 45 for men). 50.8% of the women surveyed affirm that there is greater gender discrimination in access to financing and business activities.

The main reason women-led businesses fail is lack of access to capital. LAC is the region with the second highest rate of bankruptcies of companies run by women worldwide. The main reasons for this are lack of access to credit, lack of financial education, and low profitability. Women entrepreneurs point to the difficulty of obtaining financing as the main barrier to their business growth. In addition, 70% of women entrepreneurs in LAC have an average annual income of $9,826.

The Global Banking Alliance ensures that women clients show a stronger saving behavior (16%) more likely than men for savings, with lower risk (on average, they are more responsible and disciplined). They also have greater access to financial services through intermediaries that implement programs that effectively meet the needs of women see[^2].

## Women in Microfinance Business

Currently, the supply of gender-lens financial products in LAC is limited since, on the one hand, there is a lack of demand for these types of products and, on the other hand, no efforts are made to search for the growing portfolio of women-led businesses.

[^2]: Note 2

Page 11

**Page 164**
---

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund: Annex B Gender Assessment

businesses. Furniture, financial intermediaries are dominated by men, especially at decision-making levels.

Despite this, a gender-lens investing for women-led business is emerging in various types of financing. For example, the International Finance Corporation (IFC) has invested $10 million in a fund that invests in promoting gender equality in private markets. The microfinance sector has also seen an increase in commercial banks having worked with individual investors, including sophisticated angel investors and high net worth individuals, to create impact investing funds focused by women-led businesses. These banks offer specialized lines of credit and technical assistance to SMEs run by women; in addition, other investments are provided through gender-lens investing funds that focus on women's empowerment in the region, especially in Brazil, Mexico and Colombia.

## IDB's program Women Entrepreneurship Banking (weB)

facilitates access to finance for women-led businesses in LAC through advisory services and technical assistance. The program has supported 12 countries, approximately 800 women entrepreneurs with $3 million in loans and $500,000 in grants. It provides technical assistance to develop and market products and services for women's empowerment and making impressive advancements serving their female customers.

## Pro Mujer is a leading microfinance organization in Latin America

that focuses on providing financial services to low-income women. They offer a combination of financial, health, and educational services. Their integrated approach includes:

- Financial services: loans, savings, and insurance.
- Health services: preventive care, health education, and referrals.
- Educational services: business training, literacy programs, and personal development workshops.

In 2017, Pro Mujer distributed more than $35 million in loans to over 46,000 women clients. The organization is funded primarily through a commercial bank and its clients have an average loan of $800.

## Calvert Impact Capital issued a gender-focused Social Bond issue by Banco Davivienda SA (Denominated in US Dollars)

with a 7-year maturity. This is the world's first issue of a gender-focused social bond. The proceeds will be used to finance the growth of its women-led SMEs portfolio (WSMEL), as well as the expansion of other gender-lens investment strategies.

## Access to land and women territorial care

Although in the region there are some large and medium urban centers - such as Manaus, Belém or Iquitos - the majority is enormously rural. In rural areas, the predominant forms of production are based on agriculture and livestock, with a strong presence of private properties including different productive activities. However, the land tenure of women in these regions is precarious due to the lack of access to land ownership. For example, in the Amazon region, women have been historically excluded from land ownership and administration over these lands. For example, in Colombia, women's access to land


---
**Page 166**
---

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

Tenure is precious and their ownership, or even inheritance rights and shared tenure with pathers or spouses, is rarely recognized (UNP, 2017: Zone 2015).

There are different social, economic, legal and cultural factors that can explain the gender differences in land tenure security. Only 15% of women have secure land tenure and only 2% of those who speak Spanish do not even have a National Identification Card (INPI, 2018). In addition, women's participation in community enterprises is low; it is Amazonian women who assume most of the care and subsistence of their families (PROAmazonía, 2019).

However, although Amazonian women do not exercise ownership over land widely, they are active participants in the productive activities of their communities. Pastoral and Afro-descendant peoples. This is also true for indigenous peoples who have a strong relationship with the natural environment. In fact, a PROAmazonía study (2019) shows that in productive activities, around 37% of women participate. Although this percentage is lower than men's, it has been found that the use of Amazonian women's knowledge is fundamental to the sustainability of these communities.

In addition, women are also active in the productive activities of agriculture, livestock farming and fisheries in which different forms of environmental care and sustainable use are promoted. In general, women have a greater participation in agricultural production than men; they are responsible for three quarters of the work in the field (FAO, 2019). Women's participation is also higher in the collection of wild products such as fruits, nuts, medicinal plants and other non-timber forest products. In many cases, women have a high participation rate in the production of handicrafts.

Women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Some research argued that women are more affected by the effects of climate change because they are responsible for productive activities of agriculture, livestock and fishing (Montalvo, 2019). Women who live in rural areas are also more exposed to sexual violence and domestic violence. In addition, women face increased risks due to environmental degradation such as deforestation, mining and pollution (PROAmazonía, CONAMAQ, 2019:28). In addition, women face increased risks of health problems related to the use of agrochemicals and pesticides (Montalvo, 2019).

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

by deforestation. This is because, while men own land and can take advantage of free labour for timber sales or the expansion to productive activities, women benefit from the sale of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) such as fruits, nuts, and medicinal plants (Villas 2013).

This is why different organizations stress the importance of including a gender perspective in conservation and sustainable development (OTCA 2019; PROCMASSIA & ONU/Mujeres 2019). Our analysis shows that women are excluded from decision-making processes at the local level, such as the management of protected natural areas and indigenous territories. Together, these forms of existing gender inequality in the Amazon region are exacerbated by the fact that more than half of the population lives in rural areas and that women are excluded from a substantial extension of agricultural activities. In 2017, 53% of the total population lived in rural areas, with 49.8% being women (IBGE 2018). The area is Brazil with more than 50%, and is followed by Venezuela and Peru with around 60%. The Amazon region has a high concentration of indigenous territories and protected natural areas. Networks of Gendered Environmental Information, there are few areas (of the total) where women have control over their own resources.

(1) Areas of direct use in which natural resources are conserved within the framework of management plans that regulate and control the different uses of these resources. These areas include indigenous territories and protected natural areas, which cover more than 50% of the Protected Natural Areas of the Amazon (RAISG 2020). The main activities carried out by women in these areas are research, education and tourism, although human settlements are generally not considered as part of this category. These areas cover more than half of the Protected Natural Areas of the region.

This is particularly relevant because there are 2,778 indigenous territories covering more than 1 million km² (RAISG 2020). The majority of these territories are located in Brazil and Peru, with a total area of 936,540 km² and 303,341 km² respectively. Most of these indigenous territories are officially recognized and have been demarcated by the states. In addition to this, there is an estimated area of 282,813 km² that has been highlighted as having high levels of biodiversity and cultural significance (PROCMASSIA 2019). The majority of these areas are located in Brazil and Peru.

(2) Areas where women have control over their own resources. These areas include indigenous territories and protected natural areas, which cover more than half of the Protected Natural Areas of the Amazon (RAISG 2020). The main activities carried out by women in these areas are research, education and tourism, although human settlements are generally not considered as part of this category. These areas cover more than half of the Protected Natural Areas of the region.

This is particularly relevant because there are 2,778 indigenous territories covering more than 1 million km² (RAISG 2020). The majority of these territories are located in Brazil and Peru, with a total area of 936,540 km² and 303,341 km² respectively. Most of these indigenous territories are officially recognized and have been demarcated by the states. In addition to this, there is an estimated area of 282,813 km² that has been highlighted as having high levels of biodiversity and cultural significance (PROCMASSIA 2019). The majority of these areas are located in Brazil and Peru.

# The Amazon Biodiversity Fund: Annex B Gender Assessment

However, there are already multiple organizations that have strong representation and visibility of women in their leadership, such as the Coordination of Indigenous Organizations of the Amazon Basin (COICA) and the Association of Indigenous Peoples of Brazil (APIB). These high representation of women promoting indigenous rights and environmental sustainability (APIB and COICA), as well as other organizations that have played a central role in the management of different protected areas, have been recognized for their leadership. In recent years, they have actively involved in sustainable production processes and the conservation of biodiversity.

Many strategies to gain natural resources from local knowledge and conserve the tropical forest in general (Line and Vredenburg, 2017; Nasi et al., 2000). This means that areas have been identified as priority areas for conservation. The degradation and destruction of the biological resources that support the livelihoods of indigenous peoples is a major concern. Indigenous Territories are free from environmental degradation, related to threats of deforestation and mining. There are even multiple studies that point to the relationship between the deforestation of these territories and the increase in carbon emissions (Nasi et al., 2017). These communities are working for biodiversity such as carbon capture, biodiversity protection and conservation of hydrological systems (Nasi et al., 2000).

## 6. BIOECONOMY CHALLENGES TO WOMEN PARTICIPATION IN VALUE CHAINS

The Amazon faces multiple challenges associated with deforestation and environmental pollution that affect mainly the unstructured economic activities (RAISG, 2020). In recent years, there has been a growing interest in the sustainable use of natural resources. Taking advantage of biodiversity as a sustainable way to contribute to the conservation and preservation of the environment is a priority for many countries. The participation of women in community-based bioeconomy is given and sustainable economies, emerges as an innovative alternative to promote the development of these communities. It is important to understand the importance and value of keeping the tropical forests standing, not only from an environmental point of view but also from the social and economic aspects available to quantify gender gaps within current bioeconomy initiatives in the Amazon, but we need more studies that support this.

Taking a step forward in including women into bioeconomy efforts is presented upon the recognition of their contributions to the conservation of natural resources. Women are involved in value chains that manage and preserve cultural resources such as bees, fish, and other species. It is important to recognize the role of women in these processes and to include them in the development of policies that promote their participation.

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund: Annex B Gender Assessment

Knowledge of the local communities they belong to. Some of these gaps are related to ownership of productive assets (land, animals, capital), unequal distribution of the use of time in domestic and productive activities, and limited access to financial services and credit. In general, women have more difficulties in accessing productive resources such as land, investments and profits, success is determined and technology, among others; in the case of the Amazon region, these gaps are even greater due to the lack of infrastructure, remoteness of these communities, emphasis that gender equality and the empowerment of women's active inclusion in bioconomy alternatives can lead to greater social and environmental sustainability. This is the clear distinction between men and women in the use of time, their productive activities and their participation in decision-making processes.

FAO (2009)

Another of the gender gaps that stand out in the current trends of bioconomy in the Amazon region is related to the lack of access to credit for women. This is a growing trend to foster such women's role for insurance in central villages based on the experiences and knowledge of local communities, especially those of the Xavante Indigenous Territory (Teachers Place) in the Brazilian Amazon. Since 2017, these women have been achieving their own management products, distributing non-timber forest products. One of the products they have managed to produce is the Brazil nut oil, which has a high commercial value and is part of the process of collecting, washing and processing the seed (Lima and Vieira, 2019). In this sense, women's participation in the management of non-timber forest resources is important for income generation and their families. These women have also gained traditional knowledge about the use of Brazil nut oil for cooking and personal care (Lima and Vieira, 2017; FUNAI, 2019). With this, they have managed to generate other sources of income from their production and agriculture. They have managed to combine their traditional knowledge with new technologies.

It is generally one of the problems of inequality faced by women in the green economy (for bioconomy) in reaching working spaces and value chains that have traditionally been dominated by men. In this sense, it is necessary to promote gender equity in bioconomy, as it has enabled more opportunities for women in the use of non-timber forest resources. This is a clear example of how women's participation in the management of natural resources can be an important factor in promoting sustainable development and environmental conservation in Brazil's native forests. In addition, these women have been able to generate income from their activities, which has contributed to improving their quality of life.

In recent years, the Amazon region has faced a series of challenges related to knowledge and functional foods. A bioconomy that has tried to respond to these gaps is


---
**Page 170**
---

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

The case of more than 240 women in the Brazilian Amazon who process fruits like açaí, copal, among others (UN Women, 2019). Most of these women are indigenous and live in rural communities. This group of women has managed to have additional sources of income through the sale of their products. They have gained access to technical knowledge and training that allows them to participate in public settings, negotiate with other actors and achieve gender equality. They have also been able to contribute to the spread of good environmental management practices to prevent the killing (which spells economic disaster when banned). Among these women is a group from the community of Santa Rosa do Purus.

Another barrier to the participation of Amazonian women in bioeconomy is the usual bonding of peasant and riverside women in reading bio-businesses. One example is the case of the women's association of the indigenous and riverine communities in the boroughs of Pará in the Brazilian Amazon. After the 1980s, the government began to support these communities through the Funai (National Indian Foundation) and the Tubarão do Sul. These associations have been able to ensure the sustainability of açaí palm in the state of Amapá since 2000. For this reason, a group of leaders from the community has managed to create an association that ensures the economic sustainability of the communities through the sale of fruits, ensuring appreciation in the area and generating greater economic income for these women. This has been achieved thanks to the collaboration with some women but after a few years of implementation they present difficulties in maintaining their participation due to lack of support from the Women's Association. Women even participate in the program as they begin to see the benefits.

On the other hand, some international institutions have identified that one of the main barriers to women's participation in the green economy is that they tend to spend many hours on domestic chores and care for their children (UN Women, 2019). In economic development (ICED, 4.1), the Amazon Region 10 States Development Cooperation (SDSN-Amazonia) has identified that women are responsible for 60% of the work in the household (PRD/Unicamp, 2018). It is also noted that women have a greater participation in the care economy and domestic activities. This prevents them from participating more actively in the green economy.

Finally, one of the factors affecting women's participation in bioeconomy is the uneven distribution of land ownership among Amazonian women who have access to land ownership, which affects their decision-making.

**Page 171**

# The Amazon Bioconomy Fund
## Annex B Gender Assessment

power over objects and forms of production (central elements in sustainable production of the household) (Pinedo-Vasquez, 2019). In addition, the generation of an unequal distribution of power between men and women is evident. The majority of Amazonian women, for their part, tend to have less economic power because they do not own land or property. This situation is aggravated by the fact that the Ministry of Women and Gender Equity (Ministerio de la Mujer y Equidad de Género) does not have a department at the provincial level; there is only one in Lima. There is also a wage gap of almost 30% between men and women (Cifuentes, 2019). The lack of recognition of the work of Amazonian women, access to land and capital, implies that women have fewer assets to invest.

Access to credit is crucial for the Amazonian family farmer. There is a problem of access to financial services in rural areas due to the high costs of transportation and the lack of infrastructure (World Bank, 2014). The Amazon region has a low level of formal employment and self-employment. According to the National Institute of Statistics and Informatics (INEI), only 38% of the economically active population is formally employed in the region.

In sum, the participation of Amazonian women in bioconomy alternatives still presents multiple gaps linked to pre-existing inequality conditions of women's livelihoods and their productive activities. The majority of Amazonian women tend to have more precarious conditions than men. Specifically, they are less likely to be owners or co-owners of land (INEI, 2019). According to the Censo Agropecuario (Agricultural Census) conducted in February 2012 by INEI, only 12.84% of women access employment (Non-agricultural activities) in the region.

### 7. STAKEHOLDER SOCIALIZATION PHASE I: GENDER OUTCOMES

**Summary of the main findings**

- **SFPs** of all expressed concerns in the Amazon are linked to the protection of the territory.
- Protection-related comments are more common amongst men than women (gender).
- Women talk more about citizen initiatives aimed to solve the issue in the region, especially in Brazil. These initiatives could be Rainforest Guardians - forming groups to protect or food baskets.
- In Brazil and Peru, women are experiencing increasing trends of food insecurity.
- In Colombia, women are concerned with the protection of their territory, which is linked to the protection of their livelihoods (food security).
- Bio-diversities are being threatened in Amazon countries. Some of them have a gender dimension, such as the protection of the forest and the control of the land by indigenous communities, who are the custodians between development, by companies, and indigenous associations.
- In Peru, women are more concerned about the protection of their territory (land) and their livelihoods (fishing partnership) along to position Guyana as a bed tourism destination.
- In Brazil, women are more concerned with the protection of their territory (forest), especially in the Amazon region. Native women see it as the source for their economic activities, including sustainable fishing. If they lose this, they will be abandoned fishing.
- Some specific women findings
  - In Peru, citizen discussion is closely linked to women. There are numerous organizations of women that are aimed to defend their territories. For instance, a group of women

# The Amazon Biodiversity Fund
## Gender and Diversity Action Plan

The Gender and Diversity Action Plan aims to undertake comprehensive activities with regard to gender and inclusion of indigenous peoples. Also, it will be implemented in collaboration with other organizations that have similar objectives.

### Objectives

- **Objective 1:** To ensure the participation of women in all phases of the project.
- **Objective 2:** To promote the development of skills for women in the field of biodiversity conservation and sustainable use of natural resources.
- **Objective 3:** To create an enabling environment that promotes gender equality, including the integration of gender issues into the planning and implementation of activities.

### Activities

| ACTIVITIES | INDICATIONS AND TARGETS | TIMELINE | RESPONSIBILITIES | COST |
|------------|-------------------------|----------|------------------|------|
| Impact Assessment | Contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation in the Amazon, including those owned or led by women, indigenous peoples, all dependencies, and partner communities. | 2021 | Gender Action Plan Team | $500 |
| Gender Budgeting | Improve resource skills for businesses to participate in value chains and financial services. 90% of funds earmarked will be used for training and capacity building. | 2021 | Gender Action Plan Team | $10,000 |

### Gender Action Plan

- **Activity 1:** Training on gender equality and inclusion.
- **Activity 2:** Development of a gender-responsive budget.
- **Activity 3:** Monitoring and evaluation.

**Timeline:**
- **Activity 1:** By the end of 2021 is ready to be implemented.
- **Activity 2:** By the end of 2021 is ready to be implemented.
- **Activity 3:** Ongoing throughout the project.

**Responsibilities:**
- Gender Action Plan Team

**Costs:**
- $500 for Impact Assessment
- $10,000 for Gender Budgeting
